- **What it is**: middleware that resolves the current request's tenant (from a claim or a header), publishes it on the scoped [ITenantContext](group-05-cqrs-pipeline.md#itenantcontext), and rejects a request that has none when tenancy is required.
- **Depends on**: [ITenantContext](group-05-cqrs-pipeline.md#itenantcontext), [TenancySettings](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#tenancysettings) (via `IOptions<T>`), and `IProblemDetailsService` (`TenantResolutionMiddleware.cs:51-55`). The persistence layer's query filter, save interceptor and per-tenant database routing all read the context this middleware sets (`:10-12`).
- **Concept introduced: request-scoped tenant resolution that fails closed.** `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses whether data isolation holds by default: an unscoped request in a multi-tenant database reads across every tenant, which is the exact outcome tenancy exists to prevent, so with `Tenancy:RequireTenant` (the default) an unresolved tenant is answered `400 Bad Request` rather than allowed through (class remarks, `TenantResolutionMiddleware.cs:28-34`). `[Rubric §8, Data Architecture]` applies because the value published here is what scopes the EF global filter. `[Rubric §17, DevOps & Deployment]`: hosts that never opted in pay nothing, since `IOptions<TenancySettings>` resolves to defaults with `Enabled` false and the middleware passes every request straight through (`:15-20`).
- **Walkthrough**
  - `UnresolvedTenantTitle` (`TenantResolutionMiddleware.cs:40`): the `internal const` problem title, shared with the tests.
  - `InvokeAsync(HttpContext, ITenantContext, IOptions<TenancySettings>, IProblemDetailsService)` (`:51-55`): per-invoke injection, null-guarding its first three arguments (`:57-59`).
  - Fast exit (`:63-67`): if `!settings.Enabled` or `IsExcluded(...)`, call `next` and return. `IsExcluded` (`:90-95`) matches `settings.EffectiveExcludedPathPrefixes` with `StartsWithSegments` under `OrdinalIgnoreCase`, which is how health, liveness and discovery endpoints keep answering before any tenant exists.
  - Resolution (`:69-74`): `Resolve(...)` returns the first non-blank candidate, and on success the tenant is published with `tenantContext.SetTenant(tenantId)` (`:71`) before the pipeline continues.
  - `Resolve` (`:106-123`) walks `settings.EffectiveResolutionOrder` and switches on the strategy: `Claim` reads `context.User?.FindFirst(settings.ClaimType)?.Value` (`:112`), `Header` reads `settings.HeaderName` (`:113`), and `Host` deliberately yields `null` (`:114`) because it is declared but not implemented and options validation refuses to start a host that selected it (`:101-105`). Candidates are trimmed (`:119`).
  - Opt-out (`:76-82`): with `RequireTenant` false an unresolved request runs as a system caller and sees every tenant's rows, which the comment marks as only correct behind an internal boundary (`:78-79`).
  - `RejectAsync` (`:132-155`) sets 400 (`:137`) and writes a problem document that names the configured claim and header (`:146-150`), so the caller can fix the request without reading the source. Its doc comment (`:125-131`) states that both are named regardless of `EffectiveResolutionOrder`, so the detail can name a source that `Resolve` did not consult (for example a header when the order is claim-only).
- **Why it's built this way**: see [ADR-073](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/073-multi-tenancy-model.html). The registration position is load-bearing: it runs immediately after `UseAuthentication()` because the claim strategy reads `HttpContext.User`, which has no token claims until authentication has run (`:21-26`). Registering it earlier would silently demote every request to the header strategy, so the adjacency is not left to convention: [MiddlewarePipelineBuilder](#middlewarepipelinebuilder)`.Build()` enforces it with `RequireImmediatelyBefore(Authentication, TenantResolution, ...)` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/Pipeline/MiddlewarePipelineBuilder.cs:267-270`), so a host that reorders the pipeline fails at startup rather than misrouting tenants.
- **Where it's used**: the `TenantResolution` step of the default pipeline (`MiddlewarePipelineBuilder.cs:106-112`), which [WebApplicationExtensions](#webapplicationextensions)`.UseCommonMiddlewarePipeline()` applies. It is wired unconditionally and inert by default.

### UnhandledResultFailureFilter
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Middleware` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Middleware/UnhandledResultFailureFilter.cs:22` · Level 4 · class (sealed, partial)

- **What it is**: a globally registered `IAlwaysRunResultFilter` that catches an action which returned a failed [Result](group-01-result-error-handling.md#result) inside an `Ok(...)` (or any `ObjectResult`) and rewrites the response as a proper problem document at the right status code.
- **Depends on**: [Result](group-01-result-error-handling.md#result), [Error](group-01-result-error-handling.md#error), [ErrorHttpMapping](#errorhttpmapping), [IErrorLocalizer](#ierrorlocalizer); `Microsoft.AspNetCore.Mvc.Filters.IAlwaysRunResultFilter` (`UnhandledResultFailureFilter.cs:1-7`).
- **Concept introduced: the always-run result filter as a safety net.** A regular result filter can be bypassed on short-circuit paths; `IAlwaysRunResultFilter` runs for every result MVC is about to execute. `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]` assesses whether a domain failure can leak as `200 OK` with error JSON in the body (a client would treat it as success), which the class doc names as the exact defect it prevents (`UnhandledResultFailureFilter.cs:11-21`). `[Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]` assesses defense in depth: this filter converts a class of controller mistakes into a correct response plus a warning log instead of a silent wrong answer.
- **Walkthrough**: `OnResultExecuting` (`UnhandledResultFailureFilter.cs:26`):
  - The guard `context.Result is not ObjectResult { Value: Result result } || result.IsSuccess` (`:28-31`) makes the filter a no-op for everything except an `ObjectResult` carrying a failed `Result`. Note the pattern matches the base `Result`, so `Result<T>` values are caught too.
  - Logs at `Warning` with the action's display name and the errors (`:33`, message template at `:57-59`), which is how an operator learns *which* action leaked.
  - Derives the status through [ErrorHttpMapping](#errorhttpmapping)`.GetStatusCode(result.Errors)` when there is at least one error (`:35-37`), so the code reflects the most severe error present, matching `ApiControllerBase.HandleFailure` exactly (`:17-19`); with no errors at all it falls back to 500, since a failure with nothing to report is a framework-level anomaly rather than a client error.
  - Builds a `ProblemDetails` titled `"Unhandled result failure"` whose detail names the defect in plain words (`:39-44`).
  - Resolves [IErrorLocalizer](#ierrorlocalizer) from `HttpContext.RequestServices` with `GetService` (`:46`, so a host without localization simply passes null) and attaches the localized `errors` extension (`:47`).
  - Replaces `context.Result` with an `ObjectResult(problemDetails) { StatusCode = statusCode }` (`:49`).
  - `OnResultExecuted` (`:53-55`) is intentionally empty: there is nothing to do after the response ran.
- **Why it's built this way**: the logging uses the `[LoggerMessage]` source generator (`:57-63`) with a small private instance forwarder (`:65-66`) so the filter's primary-constructor `logger` is used without an allocation per call. The class is `partial` for that generator.
- **Where it's used**: added as a global MVC filter inside `AddAPI(...)` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/DependencyInjection.cs:50`), so it applies to every controller action in every host.

### ViewAsEndedMarker
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Middleware` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Middleware/ViewAsEndedMarker.cs:32` · Level 5 · class (internal static, partial)

- **What it is**: the shared-cache "view-as grant ended" marker for a multi-service host. Only the service that holds the grant store (`ViewAs:HostsGrantStore`) can read a grant row; every other service checks this marker in the distributed cache they all share (class summary, `ViewAsEndedMarker.cs:15-20`).
- **Depends on**: [ICacheService](group-09-caching.md#icacheservice), [CrossServiceCacheKeys](group-05-cqrs-pipeline.md#crossservicecachekeys), [ViewAsGrant](group-08-auth.md#viewasgrant) (for `MaxLifetime`), `IOptionsMonitor<JwtBearerOptions>` and `TimeProvider`, all resolved from the request's services (`ViewAsEndedMarker.cs:1-11`).
- **Concept**: the same pattern as [SoftDeletedUserCache](group-08-auth.md#softdeletedusercache), applied to support access. `[Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]` assesses whether a security decision still holds once modules run as separate services: ending a view on the store host must take effect on every service on the next request, not at token expiry ([ADR-137](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/137-read-only-view-as-support-access.html)). `[Rubric §11, Security]` applies to the lifetime: the marker must outlive every token the services still accept.
- **Walkthrough**
  - `KeyFor(Guid grantId)` (`ViewAsEndedMarker.cs:41`) builds `{CrossServiceCacheKeys.Prefix}viewas:ended:{grantId:D}`. It is a cross-service key because the store host writes it and every other service reads it, so it must not carry any one host's namespace (`:34-40`).
  - `TryGetTokenExpiry(ClaimsPrincipal, out DateTimeOffset)` (`:47-58`) parses the `exp` claim as invariant-culture Unix seconds and accepts it only within `0..DateTimeOffset.MaxValue` seconds, so a malformed claim never throws.
  - `WriteAsync(IServiceProvider?, ClaimsPrincipal, Guid, CancellationToken)` (`:79`): returns without writing when the services or the cache are absent (`:85-88`), computes the lifetime as the token's remaining life plus the bearer clock skew (`:90-91`) and writes `true` under `KeyFor(grantId)` (`:95`). A failed write (other than cancellation) is logged through the `[LoggerMessage]` error `LogMarkerWriteFailed` (`:97-102`, `:124-125`), not thrown: the grant row is already ended and a service that cannot reach the cache refuses every view-as request anyway (`:71-78`).
  - `RemainingTokenLife` (`:106-114`) is `expiresAt - now`, floored at zero, or `ViewAsGrant.MaxLifetime` when the token carries no expiry (`:110`).
  - `ClockSkew(IServiceProvider)` (`:117-122`) reads `TokenValidationParameters.ClockSkew` from the `JwtBearerDefaults.AuthenticationScheme` options and falls back to `TokenValidationParameters.DefaultClockSkew` (the library's 5 minutes, `:71-75`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the marker lives for the token's remaining life plus the skew because a service accepts the token until its expiry plus that skew; a marker that lapsed first would let the ended token through again (`:61-65`). The class is `internal` because the writer and the reader both live in this package (`:28-30`), and the end endpoint is the only writer, since it is the only path that ends a grant before expiry (`:22-27`).
- **Where it's used**: written by the `view-as/end` action of [AuthControllerBase](#authcontrollerbase) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Controllers/AuthControllerBase.cs:405`); read by [ViewAsGuardMiddleware](#viewasguardmiddleware) through `KeyFor` and `TryGetTokenExpiry` (`ViewAsGuardMiddleware.cs:186`, `:213`); `ClockSkew` is also reused by [SoftDeletedUserMiddleware](#softdeletedusermiddleware) to size its own marker (`SoftDeletedUserMiddleware.cs:188`).

### ViewAsGuardMiddleware
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Middleware` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Middleware/ViewAsGuardMiddleware.cs:42` · Level 6 · class (sealed, partial)

- **What it is**: the HTTP half of read-only view-as. For a request whose principal carries a view-as token (`act_sub`), it refuses every method other than GET, HEAD and OPTIONS with 403 (with two exemptions), and refuses with 401 a token whose grant is no longer live. Every other request passes after one claim lookup (class summary, `ViewAsGuardMiddleware.cs:13-33`).
- **Depends on**: [IViewAsService](group-08-auth.md#iviewasservice) (resolved only for a view-as request), [ViewAsSettings](group-08-auth.md#viewassettings) via `IOptions<T>`, [ICacheService](group-09-caching.md#icacheservice), [AuthClaimTypes](group-08-auth.md#authclaimtypes), [ViewAsEndEndpointAttribute](#viewasendendpointattribute), [ViewAsReadOnlyEndpointAttribute](group-08-auth.md#viewasreadonlyendpointattribute) and [ViewAsEndedMarker](#viewasendedmarker).
- **Concept introduced: a fail-closed guard, and why it differs from its neighbor.** `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses whether a support session can act in someone else's name: several writes never pass through a command (sign-in, refresh, password and preference changes, device registration), so the [ViewAsCommandDecorator<TCommand, TResult>](group-05-cqrs-pipeline.md#viewascommanddecoratortcommand-tresult) alone cannot keep the session read-only, and this middleware is the first layer (`:17-21`, `:31-32`). `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]` assesses how an outage degrades: unlike [SoftDeletedUserMiddleware](#softdeletedusermiddleware), the grant check fails CLOSED, because the exposure is the other way round (an outage here costs one staff user's support session, while failing open there would cost every user's every request) (`:34-40`, `:152-160`). See [ADR-137](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/137-read-only-view-as-support-access.html).
- **Walkthrough**: `InvokeAsync(HttpContext, ILogger<ViewAsGuardMiddleware>)` (`ViewAsGuardMiddleware.cs:53`):
  - Not a view-as principal (`:58-62`): call `next` and return.
  - View-as switched off or never configured (`:66-72`): when `ViewAsSettings` is unresolvable or `Enabled` is false, log and answer 401, so a token minted while view-as was on is honored for nothing.
  - Method rule (`:74-79`): a request that is not GET, HEAD or OPTIONS (`IsSafeMethod`, `:106-107`), not a POST to an action carrying [ViewAsEndEndpointAttribute](#viewasendendpointattribute) (`:109-111`), and not a POST to an endpoint marked [ViewAsReadOnlyEndpointAttribute](group-08-auth.md#viewasreadonlyendpointattribute) (`:117-119`; gRPC is always POST) is answered 403.
  - Grant check (`:81-85`), via `IsGrantLiveOnThisHostAsync` (`:94-104`), which branches on `ViewAs:HostsGrantStore`. On the store host it resolves `IViewAsService` (no service means refuse), parses the grant, actor and subject claims (`:127-133`) and calls `IsGrantActiveAsync(grantId, staffUserId, targetUserId, ...)` (`:137`). On a service without the store, `IsGrantLiveWithoutTheStoreAsync` (`:161-201`) requires the same three claims plus a usable `exp` (`TryReadTokenWithoutTheStore`, `:207-214`), refuses an expired token (`:169-174`), refuses when no `ICacheService` is registered (`:176-181`), and reads the ended marker with `GetFromSharedStoreAsync<bool?>(ViewAsEndedMarker.KeyFor(grantId), ...)` (`:185-187`), refusing when it is set (`:188-192`). Any lookup exception other than cancellation is logged and refused (`:139-143`, `:196-200`). A failed check answers 401.
  - Otherwise `next` runs (`:87`).
  - Seven `[LoggerMessage]` methods (`:216-235`) name each refusal: expired token, no shared cache, grant ended, write refused, view-as disabled, malformed token and lookup failed.
- **Why it's built this way**: `IViewAsService` is resolved from the request's services only for a view-as request, so an ordinary request resolves nothing and a host without it fails closed (`:48-52`). The grant lookup is one primary-key read and is not cached, because a cached "live" answer on one replica would outlive an End handled on another (`:37-39`); for the same reason the marker is read from the shared store, never a replica's local copy (`:158-160`). The middleware is always in the pipeline, enabled or not, so switching view-as off takes effect on tokens already issued.
- **Where it's used**: the `ViewAsGuard` step of the default pipeline (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/Pipeline/MiddlewarePipelineBuilder.cs:126-133`), after `SoftDeletedUserFilter` (`:122-124`) and before `Authorization` (`:135-137`): after authentication so `act_sub` is on the principal, and after routing so the end action's metadata is readable (`:128-132`). The step name is `MiddlewarePipelineStepNames.ViewAsGuard` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/Pipeline/MiddlewarePipelineStepNames.cs:65`).

### SoftDeletedUserMiddleware
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Middleware` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Middleware/SoftDeletedUserMiddleware.cs:40` · Level 9 · class (sealed, partial)

- **What it is**: middleware that rejects an authenticated request with 401 when the calling user has been soft-deleted or locked (BR-133), by reading a deleted-or-locked marker from the shared cache store, and (on a host that registers the validator) falling back to a database query when no marker is set. A "not deleted" answer is never cached.
- **Depends on**: [ICurrentUserService](group-08-auth.md#icurrentuserservice), [ICacheService](group-09-caching.md#icacheservice), [ISoftDeletedUserValidator](group-08-auth.md#isoftdeleteduservalidator) and [ITokenService](group-08-auth.md#itokenservice) (both resolved lazily), [SoftDeletedUserCache](group-08-auth.md#softdeletedusercache) for the key and the marker duration (`SoftDeletedUserMiddleware.cs:67-71`), and [ViewAsEndedMarker](#viewasendedmarker) for the host's JWT bearer clock skew (`:188`).
- **Concept introduced: a fail-open security check, and why.** `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses whether revoked principals stop being served, and `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]` assesses how a dependency outage degrades. The class remarks (`SoftDeletedUserMiddleware.cs:25-39`) document the trade-off explicitly: this check runs on the hot path of every authenticated request, so failing closed would turn any cache or database blip into a total outage. Failing open is bounded instead, because access tokens live 15 minutes and the deletion or lock already revoked the refresh token, so no new access token can be minted. The residual exposure is one already-issued token's remaining lifetime, and only while a dependency is unhealthy. The marker means "deleted or locked": an erasure, an administrator's lock or a machine-client revocation writes it, and an unlock clears it (class summary, `:13-16`). The validator query checks deletion only, so a lock is enforced here by the marker alone, and after the marker expires by the sign-in and refresh gates (`:17-20`). `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]` covers the cache: the default marker lives 20 minutes (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Auth/SoftDeletedUserCache.cs:41`), the default 15-minute access-token lifetime plus the library's 5-minute bearer clock skew (`SoftDeletedUserCache.cs:32-35`), so it outlives any token issued before the delete or lock. The trade is that a live user costs one shared-store read, plus one validator query on the Identity host, per authenticated request (class comment, `SoftDeletedUserMiddleware.cs:20-22`). This middleware is the fail-open counterpart of [ViewAsGuardMiddleware](#viewasguardmiddleware), which fails closed on the same kind of outage.
- **Walkthrough**: `InvokeAsync(HttpContext, ICurrentUserService, ICacheService, ILogger<SoftDeletedUserMiddleware>)` (`SoftDeletedUserMiddleware.cs:67-71`), per-invoke injected including the logger (the reason is stated at `:62-65`):
  - Anonymous fast path (`:75-83`): no `UserId`, call `next` and return. The comment notes this is the common case in extracted services, which see internal gRPC/HTTP traffic with no user.
  - Cache read (`:85-106`): key from `SoftDeletedUserCache.KeyFor(userId.Value)` (`:85`). The read is `GetFromSharedStoreAsync<bool?>` (`:94-96`), never the replica's local L1 copy, because the marker is written by whichever replica handled the delete and a local read could miss it for the local cache duration (`:91-93`). A cache exception that is not an `OperationCanceledException` is logged and treated as a miss (`:98-106`), and `cacheReachable` goes false so the write is skipped too.
  - `cachedResult is true` (`:108-115`): the user is known deleted or locked, respond 401 and stop the pipeline. This happens before the validator is looked up, so the marker is honored on every host, including one that does not host Identity.
  - Validator resolution (`:117-125`): `context.RequestServices.GetService<ISoftDeletedUserValidator>()`. The validator is implemented by the Identity module, so a service that does not host Identity has none registered; lazy resolution lets the middleware no-op there instead of 500-ing every request (remarks at `:51-61`). On such a host a cache miss or unreachable cache simply passes the request through. This is the "wired unconditionally, inert when not applicable" convention that [TenantResolutionMiddleware](#tenantresolutionmiddleware) later copied by name (`TenantResolutionMiddleware.cs:16`).
  - `cachedResult is false` (`:127-134`): an explicit "not deleted" entry, written only by an earlier framework version; it is honored because it came from the shared store, where a later delete overwrites it with the marker.
  - No marker (`:136-153`): query `IsUserSoftDeletedAsync` (`:142-144`). If that throws (again excluding cancellation) the request is allowed through with a warning (`:146-152`), which is the fail-open path.
  - A deleted user (`:155-176`): the marker is written with `SetAsync(cacheKey, true, MarkerDurationFor(context.RequestServices), ...)` when the cache is reachable (`:157-172`, a failed write only costs the next request a lookup), then 401 (`:174-175`). A live answer is never written back, because each replica keeps its own local copy of a cached value and a cached live answer would outlive a delete made on another replica (`:136-138`).
  - Otherwise `next` runs (`:178`).
  - `MarkerDurationFor(IServiceProvider?)` (`:186-189`): when an [ITokenService](group-08-auth.md#itokenservice) is registered, the lifetime is `SoftDeletedUserCache.MarkerDurationFor(tokens.AccessTokenLifetime, ViewAsEndedMarker.ClockSkew(services))`, which is the access-token lifetime plus the bearer clock skew and never less than the 20-minute default (`SoftDeletedUserCache.cs:51-55`); with no token service the lifetime is unknown on this host, so `SoftDeletedUserCache.MarkerDuration` is written (`:181-185`).
  - Three `[LoggerMessage]` warnings (`:191-213`) name the failure precisely: cache read (which says it falls back to the validator query when one is registered), cache write, validator, each stating what the request did next.
- **Why it's built this way**: every `catch` filters out `OperationCanceledException` (`:98`, `:146`, `:166`), so a client disconnect is never misread as a dependency outage and is left to [OperationCanceledExceptionHandler](#operationcanceledexceptionhandler). Returning a bare 401 status with no body (`:113`, `:174`) is deliberate: a revoked principal gets no detail to work with. Sizing the marker from the host's own token lifetime and skew means a host that raises either still writes a marker that outlasts its tokens.
- **Where it's used**: the `SoftDeletedUserFilter` step of the default pipeline (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/Pipeline/MiddlewarePipelineBuilder.cs:122-124`), placed after `RateLimiting` (`:114-120`) and before `ViewAsGuard` (`:126-133`) and `Authorization` (`:135-137`), so it runs only once a principal exists and before any policy grants access. The step name is `MiddlewarePipelineStepNames.SoftDeletedUserFilter` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/Pipeline/MiddlewarePipelineStepNames.cs:59`).

### ApiParameterDescriptorBackfillProvider
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.OpenApi` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/OpenApi/ApiParameterDescriptorBackfillProvider.cs:43` · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: a defensive `IApiDescriptionProvider` that walks every API description MVC produced and fills in a placeholder `ParameterDescriptor` wherever the API explorer left one null, so an OpenAPI transformer that dereferences the property cannot turn document generation into a 500.
- **Depends on**: `Microsoft.AspNetCore.Mvc.Abstractions.ParameterDescriptor` and `Microsoft.AspNetCore.Mvc.ApiExplorer.IApiDescriptionProvider` (ASP.NET Core), nothing first-party. Registered by `AddCommonApiVersioning()` and `AddCommonOpenApi()` on [WebApplicationBuilderExtensions](#webapplicationbuilderextensions).
- **Concept introduced: the API-explorer provider chain as a repair point.** MVC builds its OpenAPI input (the `ApiDescription` list) by running a chain of `IApiDescriptionProvider` instances; `OnProvidersExecuting` runs in ascending `Order`, `OnProvidersExecuted` in **descending** order, so the lowest order gets the last word (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/OpenApi/ApiParameterDescriptorBackfillProvider.cs:34-41`). That makes the chain a legitimate place to normalize data other components will read. `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]` assesses whether the machine-readable contract is dependable: here the whole point is that `GET /openapi/{documentName}.json` must not fail for a consumer that adopts URL-segment versioning. `[Rubric §32, Dependency & Supply-Chain]` assesses what a framework does about defects in packages it forces on its consumers: this is a local guard around a third-party bug, written so it can be deleted without a behavior change once upstream adds the null check.
- **Walkthrough**
  - `Order => int.MinValue` (`ApiParameterDescriptorBackfillProvider.cs:46`): last to observe the results, so it sees the parameters `VersionedApiDescriptionProvider` contributed as well as MVC's own.
  - `OnProvidersExecuting` (`ApiParameterDescriptorBackfillProvider.cs:49-53`) is intentionally empty, and the comment says why (`:51-52`): the descriptions this guard repairs do not exist yet at that point.
  - `OnProvidersExecuted` (`ApiParameterDescriptorBackfillProvider.cs:56`) null-guards the context (`:58`), flattens `context.Results.SelectMany(description => description.ParameterDescriptions)` (`:60`) and applies `parameter.ParameterDescriptor ??= new ParameterDescriptor { ... }` (`:65-71`). The `??=` is the load-bearing operator: an existing descriptor (a `ControllerParameterDescriptor`, which carries the `ParameterInfo` that XML-comment lookups prefer) is never replaced, and the comment states that rule explicitly (`:62-64`). The synthesized replacement takes `parameter.Name ?? string.Empty` (`:67`), and the type falls back through `parameter.Type`, then `ModelMetadata?.ModelType`, then `typeof(string)` (`:68-70`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the class remarks (`ApiParameterDescriptorBackfillProvider.cs:12-42`) document the exact failure it guards. MVC leaves `ParameterDescriptor` null for a route-template token with no matching action parameter (normal for `[Route("api/v{version:apiVersion}/orders")]` or `[Route("api/{tenant}/orders")]`), while `Asp.Versioning.OpenApi` 10.2.1 reads `arg.ParameterDescriptor.Name` without a null check (`:21-27`). The fix is deliberately general (any unbound route token, not just the version token) so a `{tenant}` or `{region}` segment is covered by the same guard (`:28-33`).
- **Where it's used**: registered once through the private `AddApiParameterDescriptorBackfill()` helper (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.cs:131-133`), which uses `TryAddEnumerable` so calling both `AddCommonApiVersioning()` (`WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.cs:39`, which calls the helper at `:261`) and `AddCommonOpenApi()` (`:403`, calling at `:406`) still yields exactly one instance. The de-duplication rationale is on the helper's own doc (`:411-417`).

### ErrorResources
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Resources` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Resources/ErrorResources.cs:9` · Level 0 · class (sealed)

- **What it is**: an empty marker class whose only job is to be a `typeof(...)` anchor for the framework's own translation files, `ErrorResources.resx` and `ErrorResources.es.resx`, which sit beside it in `Resources/`. It has no members: `public sealed class ErrorResources;` at `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Resources/ErrorResources.cs:9` is the entire declaration, written in the body-less semicolon form.
- **Depends on**: nothing, first-party or otherwise. It has no `using` directives and no base type. Dependencies point the other way: [ErrorResourceSource](#errorresourcesource) wraps the `IStringLocalizer` built from this type, and [ErrorLocalizer](#errorlocalizer) (the default [IErrorLocalizer](#ierrorlocalizer)) enumerates those sources.
- **Concept introduced (the resource anchor type).** .NET's `IStringLocalizerFactory.Create(Type)` locates a resource set from a type's assembly plus its namespace-relative name, so a `.resx` needs a co-located type to point at even when that type has no behavior of its own. Keeping the anchor a real, public, empty class makes the resource set addressable in a refactor-safe way (rename the class and the resx alongside it, and no magic base-name string goes stale) and gives every module a one-line pattern to copy. Tag `[Rubric §27, i18n]`, which assesses whether user-facing text is externalized and culture-resolved rather than hard-coded: the fifteen entries in each of the two resx files are keyed by the stable machine error `Code` (`"PhoneNumber.Empty"`, `"Money.CurrencyMismatch"`, `"DateRange.Invalid"`, and so on), never by the English message, so a translation survives any rewording of the domain's fallback text. Tag `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]`, which assesses whether the API's error shape is stable across clients: the localized text moves while the `Code` a client branches on stays invariant. Tag `[Rubric §3, Clean Architecture]`, which assesses whether concerns sit in the right layer: this anchor and its resx live in the Presentation assembly `MMCA.Common.API`, so the Domain and Application layers that produce [Error](group-01-result-error-handling.md#error) values never take a localization dependency.
- **Walkthrough**: there is nothing to trace inside the type; all of its meaning is in the doc comment and in the two resx siblings. What is worth walking is the three-step path the anchor sits on.
  1. **Registration.** `AddErrorLocalization()` calls `services.AddLocalization()`, registers `ErrorLocalizer` behind `IErrorLocalizer` with `TryAddSingleton`, and then contributes the framework's own set with `services.AddErrorResources<ErrorResources>()` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/DependencyInjection.cs:126-128`). `AddErrorLocalization()` is called automatically from `AddAPI` (`DependencyInjection.cs:113`), so no host has to opt in.
  2. **Source construction.** The generic `AddErrorResources<TResource>()` registers a singleton factory that resolves `IStringLocalizerFactory`, calls `Create(typeof(TResource))`, and wraps the result in an [ErrorResourceSource](#errorresourcesource) (`DependencyInjection.cs:144-148`). `ErrorResources` is just the first `TResource`; each module passes its own anchor, which is why translations are additive rather than an edit to a framework file.
  3. **Lookup.** [ErrorLocalizer](#errorlocalizer) snapshots every registered source in registration order (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Localization/ErrorLocalizer.cs:13`, so Common's set is consulted first), returns the caller's fallback immediately for an empty code (`ErrorLocalizer.cs:21-24`), then walks the sources and returns the first hit whose `ResourceNotFound` is false (`:23-30`). A code no source knows falls through to the original English message (`:32`), so an untranslated code degrades instead of throwing or rendering a key.
- **Why it's built this way**: see [ADR-027](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/027-multi-locale-i18n.html) (multi-locale i18n). Localizing at the HTTP edge rather than inside the domain keeps `Error.Code` and the fallback message culture-free all the way through the Application layer, and one anchor per resource set turns "add Spanish for my module's errors" into a new resx plus one `AddErrorResources<T>()` call instead of a merge into shared framework resources.
- **Where it's used**: registered as the framework's own source at `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/DependencyInjection.cs:128`. The text it supplies reaches clients through three call sites that resolve `IErrorLocalizer` from the request container: [ApiControllerBase](#apicontrollerbase) when it turns a failed [Result](group-01-result-error-handling.md#result) into a response (`Controllers/ApiControllerBase.cs:57`), [UnhandledResultFailureFilter](#unhandledresultfailurefilter) (`Middleware/UnhandledResultFailureFilter.cs:46`), and [SupportsIfMatchAttribute](#supportsifmatchattribute) when it shapes a precondition failure (`Concurrency/SupportsIfMatchAttribute.cs:217`); all three hand the localizer to [ErrorHttpMapping](#errorhttpmapping)`.BuildErrorsExtension` (`Middleware/ErrorHttpMapping.cs:61`), which is where the code-to-text substitution happens. Module anchors follow the same shape: [ConferenceErrorResources](group-20-conference-api-grpc.md#conferenceerrorresources), [EngagementErrorResources](group-22-engagement-module.md#engagementerrorresources), and [IdentityErrorResources](group-24-identity-module.md#identityerrorresources).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the class file tells you nothing about which codes are covered. The key set lives in the two resx assets beside it (fifteen entries each today, matched one-for-one between English and Spanish), and nothing in this type enforces that the two files stay in sync or that every framework `Error.Code` has an entry.

### OptInActionAttribute
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Pagination` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Pagination/OptInActionAttribute.cs:17` · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: a method attribute that is also an MVC `IActionModelConvention`. Placed on a base-controller action, it removes that action from the application model of every controller that does not carry a named opt-in attribute, so the endpoint exists only where a controller asked for it.
- **Depends on**: `Microsoft.AspNetCore.Mvc.ApplicationModels` (`ActionModel`, `IActionModelConvention`; `OptInActionAttribute.cs:1`), nothing first-party. The opt-in markers it is given today are [SupportsCursorPagingAttribute](#supportscursorpagingattribute) and [SupportsChangeFeedAttribute](#supportschangefeedattribute).
- **Concept introduced (an action convention as an opt-in switch).** A generic base controller such as [EntityControllerBase](#entitycontrollerbasetentity-tentitydto-tidentifiertype) can declare an action once for every entity, but not every entity should expose it. MVC runs each `IActionModelConvention` found on an action while it builds the application model, before routing and the API explorer read that model, so a convention that deletes the action leaves no route and no API description behind. The doc states the consequence (`OptInActionAttribute.cs:10-14`): a controller that did not opt in is documented exactly as if the action did not exist, and because the convention is read off the base action, an override that does not opt in is removed as well. `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]` assesses whether the published contract matches what a resource actually supports: here an un-opted controller's OpenAPI document is unchanged rather than listing an endpoint that would answer 500.
- **Walkthrough**
  - `[AttributeUsage(AttributeTargets.Method, AllowMultiple = false, Inherited = true)]` (`OptInActionAttribute.cs:16`) and a primary constructor taking the opt-in attribute type (`:17`).
  - `OptInAttributeType` (`:20`) stores that type, throwing `ArgumentNullException` for `null`.
  - `Apply(ActionModel)` (`:24-33`) null-guards the action (`:26`), returns early when the action has no controller or when any of `controller.Attributes` is an instance of `OptInAttributeType` (`:28-30`), and otherwise calls `controller.Actions.Remove(action)` (`:32`). `IsInstanceOfType` means a marker inherited from an abstract base controller counts, since `controller.Attributes` carries inherited attributes (doc `:5-8`).
- **Why it's built this way**: one generic convention parameterized by a `Type` keeps each feature's switch a body-less public marker class while the mechanism stays `internal` (`:17`), so consumers see only the markers. Removing the action at model-build time, rather than returning 404 at request time, is what keeps the API document honest for both opted-in and un-opted controllers. See [ADR-145](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/145-opt-in-cursor-paging.html) (opt-in cursor paging) and [ADR-144](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/144-offline-writes-and-delta-reads.html) (delta reads).
- **Where it's used**: on two actions of [EntityControllerBase](#entitycontrollerbasetentity-tentitydto-tidentifiertype): `GetCursorPageAsync` with `[OptInAction(typeof(SupportsCursorPagingAttribute))]` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Controllers/EntityControllerBase.cs:403-404`) and `GetChangesAsync` with `[OptInAction(typeof(SupportsChangeFeedAttribute))]` (`EntityControllerBase.cs:456-457`). Exercised by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/Controllers/EntityControllerBaseCursorTests.cs` and `EntityControllerBaseChangeFeedTests.cs` in the same folder.

### RateLimitAlgorithm
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.RateLimiting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/RateLimiting/RateLimitAlgorithm.cs:8` · Level 0 · enum

- **What it is**: a two-value enum selecting the limiting algorithm used by the in-memory rate-limit partitions that `AddCommonRateLimiting` registers.
- **Depends on**: nothing. Read by [RateLimitingSettings](#ratelimitingsettings)`.Algorithm` and switched on inside [WebApplicationBuilderExtensions](#webapplicationbuilderextensions)`.CreateLimitedPartition`.
- **Concept (naming the window-replenishment trade).** Both values use the same one-minute window and the same partition keys, so switching between them changes only how permits are replenished (`RateLimitAlgorithm.cs:3-7`). `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]` assesses whether a throttle is understood rather than guessed: the two doc comments state the trade in one sentence each, which is what makes the setting safe to move in production.
- **Walkthrough**
  - `FixedWindow` (`RateLimitAlgorithm.cs:15`) is the default and the cheapest to run: the whole allowance becomes available again at the window boundary, which lets a caller spend the allowance twice across a boundary, once at the end of one window and once at the start of the next (`:10-14`).
  - `SlidingWindow` (`:22`) divides the one-minute window into `SegmentsPerWindow` segments and returns each segment's permits as it ages out, smoothing that boundary burst away at the cost of tracking one counter per segment (`:17-21`).
- **Why it's built this way**: an enum rather than a boolean keeps the configuration self-describing in `appsettings.json` (`"Algorithm": "SlidingWindow"`) and leaves room for a third algorithm without a breaking rename. The distributed Redis path is deliberately **not** an algorithm value: it is the separate `Distributed` flag on [RateLimitingSettings](#ratelimitingsettings), because it changes *where* the counter lives rather than how it replenishes, and [RedisFixedWindowRateLimiter](#redisfixedwindowratelimiter) implements only the fixed window.
- **Where it's used**: [RateLimitingSettings](#ratelimitingsettings)`.Algorithm` defaults it to `FixedWindow` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/RateLimiting/RateLimitingSettings.cs:49`), and `CreateLimitedPartition` branches on it (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.RateLimiting.cs:298`) to build either a `SlidingWindowRateLimiterOptions` partition (`:239-246`) or a `FixedWindowRateLimiterOptions` one (`:249-255`).

### RedisRateLimitLease
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.RateLimiting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/RateLimiting/RedisFixedWindowRateLimiter.cs:214` · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: the lease type [RedisFixedWindowRateLimiter](#redisfixedwindowratelimiter) hands back: two shared instances, `Acquired` and `Undecided`, plus a `Rejected(TimeSpan?)` factory that allocates a refusal carrying a retry-after hint.
- **Depends on**: `System.Threading.RateLimiting.RateLimitLease` (BCL abstract base) and its `MetadataName.RetryAfter` key. It lives in the same file as the limiter that produces it.
- **Concept (share the stateless leases, allocate the one that carries data).** ASP.NET Core's rate-limiting middleware asks a limiter for a `RateLimitLease` on every request and disposes it afterwards. A lease with no metadata can be one shared immutable instance; a lease that carries a value has to be allocated per call. The doc states the split directly (`RedisFixedWindowRateLimiter.cs:207-213`): `Acquired` and `Undecided` are stateless and shared by every request, while a rejection is allocated per call because it carries `MetadataName.RetryAfter`, the time until the partition's window resets, which the rate-limiter `OnRejected` callback turns into the 429's `Retry-After` header. `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]` assesses allocation on a per-request hot path: the permitted path (the common one) allocates nothing, and only a refused request pays for a lease.
- **Walkthrough**: `RetryAfterMetadataNames` (`RedisFixedWindowRateLimiter.cs:216`) is a static one-element array holding `MetadataName.RetryAfter.Name`. `Acquired = new(isAcquired: true, retryAfter: null)` (`:219`) and `Undecided = new(isAcquired: false, retryAfter: null)` (`:226`) are the two shared instances. `Undecided` is not acquired yet is kept distinct from a rejection: it is what the synchronous path returns, so the middleware falls through to the asynchronous path, and because nothing was counted and nothing was refused it carries no retry-after metadata (`:221-225`). The private constructor sets `IsAcquired` and the `_retryAfter` field (`:228`, `:230-234`). `IsAcquired` is an override with a get-only auto property (`:237`). `MetadataNames` returns an empty collection when `_retryAfter` is null and `RetryAfterMetadataNames` otherwise (`:240`). `Rejected(TimeSpan? retryAfter)` (`:250`) is the `internal static` factory for an exhausted window; its doc says a `null` hint means no later window can satisfy the request (`:242-249`). `TryGetMetadata` (`:253`) returns the `TimeSpan` and `true` only when a hint is present and the requested name ordinally equals `MetadataName.RetryAfter.Name` (`:255-260`); otherwise it nulls the out parameter and returns `false` (`:262-263`).
- **Why it's built this way**: `internal sealed` (`:214`) keeps it an implementation detail of the limiter. Every instance is immutable, so sharing `Acquired` and `Undecided` across concurrent requests is safe by construction and there is no dispose-time state to reset. Reporting the hint through the BCL's standard `MetadataName.RetryAfter` rather than a custom key is what lets the framework's generic `OnRejected` wrapper read it without knowing which limiter refused the request.
- **Where it's used**: returned by [RedisFixedWindowRateLimiter](#redisfixedwindowratelimiter) from `AttemptAcquireCore` (`:145`, always `Undecided`) and from `AcquireAsyncCore` on all three of its outcomes: the oversized request (`:159`, `Rejected(retryAfter: null)`), the counted decision (`:175-177`, `Acquired` or `Rejected(TimeUntilNextWindow(now, window))`), and the fail-open path after a Redis fault (`:186`, `Acquired`). Its hint is read by `WithRetryAfterHeader` on [WebApplicationBuilderExtensions](#webapplicationbuilderextensions) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.RateLimiting.cs:34-36`), which formats it through [RetryAfterHeader](group-16-aspire-orchestration.md#retryafterheader).

### SupportsChangeFeedAttribute
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Pagination` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Pagination/SupportsChangeFeedAttribute.cs:14` · Level 0 · class (sealed)

- **What it is**: a body-less marker attribute (`public sealed class SupportsChangeFeedAttribute : Attribute;`, `SupportsChangeFeedAttribute.cs:14`) that opts an entity controller in to the change-feed action, `GET {controller}/changes?since=&cursor=&pageSize=`, served by `EntityControllerBase.GetChangesAsync`: the rows changed at or after `since`, with soft-deleted rows returned as tombstones (doc `:3-8`).
- **Depends on**: nothing; it has no members. [OptInActionAttribute](#optinactionattribute) reads it.
- **Concept**: the change feed is the delta-read half of [ADR-144](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/144-offline-writes-and-delta-reads.html): an offline client keeps the largest `changedOn` it has seen and asks only for what moved since (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Controllers/EntityControllerBase.cs:434-455`). It is opt-in per controller through the [OptInActionAttribute](#optinactionattribute) convention, so a controller without the marker has no such endpoint and an unchanged API document (`SupportsChangeFeedAttribute.cs:7`). `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]`.
- **Walkthrough**: `[AttributeUsage(AttributeTargets.Class, AllowMultiple = false, Inherited = true)]` (`:13`). `Inherited = true` is what makes the remark at `:10` work: declared on an abstract base controller it opts in every controller derived from it.
- **Why it's built this way**: the doc remark (`:10-11`) names the dependency the marker turns on: the query service's `GetChangesSinceAsync` serves the page, and a service that predates it answers 500. Keeping the action opt-in is what stops that 500 from appearing on every entity controller by default.
- **Where it's used**: named by `[OptInAction(typeof(SupportsChangeFeedAttribute))]` on `GetChangesAsync` (`EntityControllerBase.cs:456-457`) and referenced in that action's doc (`:438`). No controller in `MMCA.Common/Source` or `MMCA.ADC/Source` carries it today; it is exercised by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/Controllers/EntityControllerBaseChangeFeedTests.cs`.

### SupportsCursorPagingAttribute
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Pagination` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Pagination/SupportsCursorPagingAttribute.cs:14` · Level 0 · class (sealed)

- **What it is**: a body-less marker attribute (`public sealed class SupportsCursorPagingAttribute : Attribute;`, `SupportsCursorPagingAttribute.cs:14`) that opts an entity controller in to the cursor (keyset) list action, `GET {controller}/cursor?sortColumn=&sortDirection=&cursor=&pageSize=`, served by `EntityControllerBase.GetCursorPageAsync` (doc `:3-8`).
- **Depends on**: nothing; it has no members. [OptInActionAttribute](#optinactionattribute) reads it.
- **Concept**: keyset ("seek") paging returns the rows after an opaque cursor, ordered by a sort column with `Id` as tie-break, so a deep page costs the same as the first one (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Controllers/EntityControllerBase.cs:387-402`). Unlike the offset-paged list there is no total, no pagination header, no field projection, no includes and no filter dictionary (`EntityControllerBase.cs:399-401`). It is opt-in per controller ([ADR-145](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/145-opt-in-cursor-paging.html)), so a controller without the marker has no such endpoint and an unchanged API document (`SupportsCursorPagingAttribute.cs:6-7`). `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]` assesses whether large reads stay bounded; `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]` applies because the endpoint is advertised only where it is served.
- **Walkthrough**: `[AttributeUsage(AttributeTargets.Class, AllowMultiple = false, Inherited = true)]` (`:13`); declared on an abstract base controller it opts in every controller derived from it (`:10`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the remark (`:10-11`) records the reason it is a switch rather than always on: the query service's `GetPageByCursorAsync` serves the page, and a service that predates it answers 500.
- **Where it's used**: named by `[OptInAction(typeof(SupportsCursorPagingAttribute))]` on `GetCursorPageAsync` (`EntityControllerBase.cs:403-404`) and referenced in that action's doc (`:390`). No controller in `MMCA.Common/Source` or `MMCA.ADC/Source` carries it today; it is exercised by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/Controllers/EntityControllerBaseCursorTests.cs`.

### RateLimitingSettings
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.RateLimiting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/RateLimiting/RateLimitingSettings.cs:21` · Level 1 · class (sealed)

- **What it is**: the options object bound from the `RateLimiting` configuration section. It carries four permit counts (global, `auth-ip`, `mcp` and anonymous hub), the algorithm choice, the sliding-window segment count, the hub path prefixes that get anonymous metering, and the flag that moves the counter into Redis.
- **Depends on**: [RateLimitAlgorithm](#ratelimitalgorithm) for the `Algorithm` property; `System.ComponentModel.DataAnnotations` for the `[Range]` attributes (`RateLimitingSettings.cs:1`). Consumed by [WebApplicationBuilderExtensions](#webapplicationbuilderextensions)`.AddCommonRateLimiting` and, through the permit limit it is handed, by [RedisFixedWindowRateLimiter](#redisfixedwindowratelimiter).
- **Concept (settings that cannot change behavior by existing).** The load-bearing property of this class is stated in its own doc comment (`RateLimitingSettings.cs:5-10`): **every** property defaults to the value the parameterized `AddCommonRateLimiting` overload has always used, so the section is optional in `appsettings.json` and a host that omits it keeps the previous behavior exactly. The doc's own example (`:11-20`) sets only `GlobalPermitLimit`, `Algorithm`, `SegmentsPerWindow` and `Distributed`. That is what made the settings-driven overload safe to add to a framework whose consumers upgrade in lockstep ([ADR-016](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/016-lockstep-versioning-masstransit-pin.html) for the sweep rule, [ADR-019](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/019-rate-limiting.html) for the limiter itself). `[Rubric §17, DevOps & Deployment]` assesses whether a cross-cutting concern is configurable without becoming mandatory; `[Rubric §17, DevOps]` applies because moving a limit is a config change rather than a release. The `[Range]` attributes make an impossible value a startup failure rather than a runtime surprise: the configuration overload binds the section with `ValidateDataAnnotations().ValidateOnStart()` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.RateLimiting.cs:407-410`), the same discipline [IdempotencySettings](#idempotencysettings) uses.
- **Walkthrough**
  - `SectionName = "RateLimiting"` (`RateLimitingSettings.cs:24`) is a `public static readonly string`, the binding key as a symbol rather than a literal repeated at the call site.
  - Three bounded, `init`-only permit counts, each `[Range(1, 1_000_000)]`: `GlobalPermitLimit` 300 per authenticated user for the always-on global limiter (`:27-28`), `AuthIpPermitLimit` 30 per client IP for the `auth-ip` policy that throttles anonymous authentication attempts (`:34-35`), and `McpPermitLimit` 60 for the `mcp` policy on the MCP endpoint, keyed per user (subject claim, then name) or, for an anonymous caller, per client IP, where one tool call is one request and a client also lists tools (`:37-43`, [ADR-138](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/138-mcp-endpoint-over-module-queries.html)). There is no queue-depth setting: every partition is built with `queueLimit: 0`.
  - `Algorithm` (`:49`) defaults to `RateLimitAlgorithm.FixedWindow`, which is what the framework has always used; `SegmentsPerWindow` (`:58`, `[Range(1, 60)]`, default 4) is the number of segments the one-minute window is divided into when the algorithm is `SlidingWindow`, and is ignored otherwise (`:51-56`).
  - `Distributed` (`:68`, default `false`) is the interesting one. Its doc (`:60-67`) states the whole contract: it makes the global limiter, the anonymous hub partition and the `mcp` policy count against a shared Redis counter instead of per-instance memory, so a limit means the same thing behind a load balancer as it does on one node; it requires an `IConnectionMultiplexer` in the container, and when none is registered the limiters **silently fall back** to the in-memory behavior rather than failing startup; and the `auth-ip` policy stays in memory either way, because per-account login protection already backs it.
  - `HubPathPrefixes` (`:87`, default `["/hubs"]`) and `AnonymousHubPermitLimit` (`:95`, `[Range(1, 1_000_000)]`, default 60) are a pair added for SEC-ADC-25 (`RateLimitingSettings.cs:70-87`, `:89-95`). The global limiter exempts anonymous callers everywhere else on purpose (public reads are output-cached, server-rendered Blazor traffic shares one host IP); a real-time hub is the exception, because a gateway that bypasses `/hubs` at the edge (ADR-024 requires it, since the hub authenticates from a query-string token the edge cannot read) left anonymous `/hubs/*/negotiate` traffic metered nowhere. `HubPathPrefixes` names which paths get that anonymous-IP metering, and `AnonymousHubPermitLimit` is deliberately generous, since one browser tab reconnecting behind a flaky network legitimately negotiates several times a minute.
- **Why it's built this way**: bundling the knobs into one bound object is what let `AddCommonRateLimiting` grow a configuration overload without changing the two-parameter one, and the "same defaults" rule means the overloads are observationally identical for a host that configures nothing (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.RateLimiting.cs:388-393`, `AddCommonRateLimiting(int globalPermitLimit = 300, int authIpPermitLimit = 30)`, builds an instance from its two parameters). The fall-back-rather-than-fail choice on `Distributed` is deliberate: a rate limiter that refuses to start is a worse outage than one that limits per instance. The `AuthIpPermitLimit` default of 30 is itself argued in the overload's parameter doc (`WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.RateLimiting.cs:380-387`): Blazor Server circuits issue the login call server-side, so every Server-circuit user shares the UI host's IP and a tighter cap would throttle real logins.
- **Where it's used**: bound in `AddCommonRateLimiting(IConfiguration)` (`WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.RateLimiting.cs:403-415`), which registers validated options for the section and builds the instance with `section.Get<RateLimitingSettings>() ?? new RateLimitingSettings()` (`:412`), then hands it to `AddCommonRateLimiting(RateLimitingSettings)` (`:425-456`). That overload threads it through every partition selector: the global limiter (`GlobalRateLimitPartition`, `:172-197`, registered at `:437-438`), the `mcp` policy (`McpRateLimitPartition`, `:209-225`, registered at `:442`, reads `McpPermitLimit` at `:221`) and the `auth-ip` policy (`AuthIpRateLimitPartition`, `:341-355`, registered at `:452-454`). Each ends in `CreateLimitedPartition` (`:259`), which reads `Distributed` (`:268`), `Algorithm` (`:298`) and `SegmentsPerWindow` (`:303`). `HubPathPrefixes` and `AnonymousHubPermitLimit` are read by `AnonymousPartition` (`WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.RateLimiting.cs:92-102`) and `IsAnonymousHubRequest` (`:112-122`, internal so `InternalsVisibleTo` lets it be unit-tested directly): an unauthenticated request that matches a hub prefix is metered per client IP through `CreateLimitedPartition` with `redisScope: "hub"` and `allowDistributed: true` (`:94-101`); every other anonymous request keeps taking `RateLimitPartition.GetNoLimiter("__anonymous")` (`:102`).

### RedisFixedWindowRateLimiter
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.RateLimiting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/RateLimiting/RedisFixedWindowRateLimiter.cs:41` · Level 1 · class (sealed, partial)

- **What it is**: a `RateLimiter` that counts a fixed one-minute window in **Redis**, so every instance behind a load balancer shares one allowance per partition instead of each getting its own.
- **Depends on**: `StackExchange.Redis.IConnectionMultiplexer`, `System.Threading.RateLimiting.RateLimiter`, `TimeProvider`, and `ILogger` with a source-generated `[LoggerMessage]` partial (`RedisFixedWindowRateLimiter.cs:1-5`). It hands back [RedisRateLimitLease](#redisratelimitlease) leases (the shared `Acquired` and `Undecided` instances, and a per-call `Rejected(TimeSpan?)`) and is selected by [RateLimitingSettings](#ratelimitingsettings)`.Distributed`.
- **Concept introduced (a distributed counter, and the three honest compromises it makes).** A per-process limiter means "300 requests a minute" actually means 300 times the replica count, which is not the number an operator configured. Moving the counter to Redis fixes that, and the type doc records the three trades it accepts to do so.
  1. **Layering** (`RedisFixedWindowRateLimiter.cs:15-22`): the limiter lives in `MMCA.Common.API` rather than behind an abstraction implemented in Infrastructure, because API already references Infrastructure (which owns the StackExchange.Redis dependency), so using the client here adds no new dependency edge and breaks no rule in `MMCA.Common.LayerEnforcement.targets`. An extra interface plus an Infrastructure implementation would buy nothing but indirection, since the limiter is a presentation concern only the rate-limiting middleware constructs. `[Rubric §3, Clean Architecture]` assesses whether a layer boundary is respected *and* whether an abstraction is added where it earns its keep; this is the second half.
  2. **Storage and precision** (`:23-30`): one key per partition per window, `rl:{partitionKey}:{unixMinute}`, incremented and given a TTL slightly longer than the window on the increment that creates it. Increment and TTL happen as one atomic server-side Lua step (`IncrementScript`, `RedisFixedWindowRateLimiter.cs:50-53`), so a fault cannot leave a counter key without an expiry. Keys expire on their own so nothing has to sweep them, and a window rollover is a new key rather than a reset. The counter is still **not transactional with the permit decision** (the script returns the count, the limiter then compares), which can let a burst arriving in the same instant overshoot the limit slightly; that is the accepted trade for one round trip per request. `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]`.
  3. **Fail open** (`:31-35`): any Redis fault permits the request and logs at warning level, at most once per window across the process. Rate limiting protects capacity, so it must never become the reason an otherwise healthy request is rejected. `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`.
- **Walkthrough**
  - Two internal constants lead the type: `IncrementScript` (`RedisFixedWindowRateLimiter.cs:50-53`), a Lua script that does `INCRBY`, and `EXPIRE` only when the new count is no more than the requested permits (the increment that created the key); and `KeyTtlSeconds = 65` (`:60`). Only the creating increment sets the TTL, because re-stamping it on every request would slide the window forward for a caller who keeps hitting it and the allowance would never reset. The 65 seconds of skew past the window covers clock drift between instances (`:55-59`).
  - `_lastLoggedFailureWindow` (`RedisFixedWindowRateLimiter.cs:67`) is **static** and initialized to `-1`: a dead Redis produces one warning a minute for the whole process rather than one per request, and the doc explains that a fault is a property of the connection, which every partition shares (`:62-66`).
  - Instance fields hold the connection, partition key, permit limit, logger and clock (`:69-73`), plus `_lastUsedTimestamp` seeded from `Stopwatch.GetTimestamp()` (`:75`).
  - The constructor (`:91`) guards all four required inputs (`:98-101`, including `ThrowIfLessThan(permitLimit, 1)`) and defaults `timeProvider` to `TimeProvider.System` (`:107`), which is the substitution point tests use to pin a window (`:87-90`).
  - `IdleDuration` (`:116-117`) reports `Stopwatch.GetElapsedTime(Volatile.Read(ref _lastUsedTimestamp))`. The doc (`:110-115`) explains why it is reported rather than left null: partition keys embed user identity, so never reporting idleness would grow the owning `PartitionedRateLimiter` table with one entry per user seen since start-up.
  - `GetStatistics()` (`:124`) returns `null` on purpose: the counter lives in Redis and reporting it would cost a round trip per call for a diagnostic the middleware does not require (`:119-123`).
  - `AttemptAcquireCore(int)` (`:142`) stamps the last-used timestamp and returns `RedisRateLimitLease.Undecided` (`:144-145`), a lease that is **not** acquired, without touching Redis. This is load-bearing (doc `:126-141`): the ASP.NET Core middleware calls the synchronous `AttemptAcquire` first on every request and calls `AcquireAsync` only when that lease is not acquired, disposing the synchronous lease without reading its metadata. Granting here would admit every request uncounted so the limit would never be enforced, and deciding here would block a request thread on a Redis round trip. The consequence the doc states: a caller that uses only the synchronous path is always refused, and the middleware is the supported consumer.
  - `AcquireAsyncCore` (`:149`) is the real path: stamp the timestamp (`:153`), then reject outright with `RedisRateLimitLease.Rejected(retryAfter: null)` when `permitCount > _permitLimit` (`:155-160`); the comment (`:157-158`) explains the missing hint: no later window can hold a request larger than the whole allowance, so telling the client when to come back would be a lie. It reads the clock once into `now` (`:162`), computes the window as `now.ToUnixTimeSeconds() / 60` (`:163`) and builds the invariant-culture key (`:164`), then runs `ScriptEvaluateAsync(IncrementScript, [key], [permitCount, KeyTtlSeconds])` (`:169-172`) and casts the result to `long` (`:173`). The increment and the conditional TTL are one atomic server-side step. The decision is the final compare (`:175-177`): `Acquired` within the limit, otherwise `Rejected(TimeUntilNextWindow(now, window))`. The catch (`:179-187`) excludes `OperationCanceledException`, logs at most once per window via `Interlocked.Exchange` on the static field (`:181-184`), and returns `Acquired` (`:186`).
  - `TimeUntilNextWindow(DateTimeOffset now, long window)` (`:198-199`, `internal static`) is the retry-after arithmetic: `DateTimeOffset.FromUnixTimeSeconds((window + 1) * 60) - now`, the time until the next window opens and the partition's allowance resets, in the same unix-minute arithmetic the key uses (doc `:190-197`). Reusing the `now` that chose the window keeps the hint consistent with the counter it was refused by.
- **Why it's built this way**: `partial` plus `[LoggerMessage]` (`:201-204`) gives a source-generated, allocation-free warning that names the partition and states plainly that requests are permitted uncounted until Redis recovers. The partition key arrives already scoped by the caller (for example `{namespace}:global:alice`, `:81-84`) so two policies limiting the same user never share one counter; that scoping is applied in `CreateLimitedPartition` as `$"{RateLimitKeyNamespace(requestServices!)}:{redisScope}:{key}"` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.RateLimiting.cs:288`), whose leading namespace (the configured `Cache:KeyPrefix` without its trailing colon, else the [ApplicationNamespace](group-14-module-system-composition.md#applicationnamespace), `:227-239`) keeps two MMCA apps on one Redis from sharing counters (SEC-Common-53, `:281-283`). The scopes in use are `global`, `hub` and `mcp` (`:192`, `:97`, `:220`).
- **Where it's used**: constructed only inside `CreateLimitedPartition` (`WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.RateLimiting.cs:284-290`), and only when `allowDistributed && settings.Distributed` is true (`:268`) **and** an `IConnectionMultiplexer` resolves (`:274-276`); the logger falls back to `NullLogger` when none is registered (`:278-279`), and `RequestServices` is read through a nullable local because it is null outside a request pipeline (`:270-273`). When no multiplexer is registered the code falls through to the in-memory limiters rather than failing startup (`:293-295`). The `auth-ip` policy passes `allowDistributed: false` (`WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.RateLimiting.cs:354`), so login throttling never depends on Redis.

### PaginationHeaderExtensions
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Pagination` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Pagination/PaginationHeaderExtensions.cs:12` · Level 1 · class (static)

- **What it is**: the one place that writes a page's `PaginationMetadata` onto the HTTP response. A static class with the header-name constant and a single `extension(HttpResponse)` member.
- **Depends on**: `PaginationMetadata` (the payload), `HttpResponse` (the extended type), and `System.Text.Json` (`JsonSerializerOptions.Web`).
- **Concept (one wire shape for every paged endpoint).** `[Rubric §3, DRY]`: the header name and its serialization options are fixed in one helper, so every paged endpoint emits the same camelCase JSON in the same header and clients parse one shape (`PaginationHeaderExtensions.cs:27-30`, doc at `:34-37`).
- **Walkthrough**
  - `HeaderName = "X-Pagination"` (`PaginationHeaderExtensions.cs:30`) is the public constant clients and tests read.
  - `AppendPaginationHeader(PaginationMetadata)` (`PaginationHeaderExtensions.cs:27-33`), declared inside an `extension(HttpResponse response)` block (`:19`), null-guards both arguments (`:29-30`) and appends `HeaderName` with the metadata serialized using `JsonSerializerOptions.Web` (`:32`).
- **Why it's built this way**: pulling the append out of individual controllers keeps the header name and casing from drifting between endpoints; the extension block lets callers write `Response.AppendPaginationHeader(metadata)`.
- **Where it's used**: the only usage site found in source is its test, `MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/Pagination/PaginationHeaderExtensionsTests.cs`. Other framework callers: not determinable from the brief's usage sites.

### RateLimitPartitions
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.RateLimiting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/RateLimiting/RateLimitPartitions.cs:11` · Level 2 · class (static)

- **What it is**: public partition factories for a host's **own** rate-limit policies, built on the same limiter `AddCommonRateLimiting` uses, so a consumer policy answers a rejection the way the framework's policies do (`RateLimitPartitions.cs:6-10`). It has one factory today, `FixedWindowWithRemainingRetryAfter<TKey>`.
- **Depends on**: `System.Threading.RateLimiting` (`RateLimitPartition`, `FixedWindowRateLimiterOptions`, `QueueProcessingOrder`) and [RemainingWindowFixedWindowRateLimiter](#remainingwindowfixedwindowratelimiter), the internal limiter compiled into this package from the linked source `MMCA.Common/Source/Build/RateLimiting/RemainingWindowFixedWindowRateLimiter.cs:31` (namespace `MMCA.Common.RateLimiting`, `RateLimitPartitions.cs:2`).
- **Concept introduced (an honest `Retry-After` for host policies).** The BCL's `RateLimitPartition.GetFixedWindowLimiter` stamps every rejection with the whole window length as its `MetadataName.RetryAfter` hint, so a 429 says "come back in 60 seconds" however much of the minute is left. The framework's own in-memory fixed window does not use it (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.RateLimiting.cs:310-312`), and this class gives a host the same partition, so a policy a consumer declares with `AddPolicy` tells the client how long it actually has to wait (doc `RateLimitPartitions.cs:13-20`). `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]` assesses whether response headers mean what they say; `[Rubric §3, DRY]` applies because the host reuses the framework's limiter instead of re-implementing the window arithmetic.
- **Walkthrough**
  - `FixedWindowWithRemainingRetryAfter<TKey>(TKey partitionKey, int permitLimit, TimeSpan window)` (`RateLimitPartitions.cs:26-27`) is the public entry point; it forwards with `timeProvider: null`.
  - The `internal` overload (`:39-57`) adds a `TimeProvider?` so tests can drive the hint (doc `:29-32`). It rejects a non-positive `permitLimit` (`ThrowIfNegativeOrZero`, `:45`) and a non-positive `window` (`:46`), then returns `RateLimitPartition.Get(partitionKey, _ => new RemainingWindowFixedWindowRateLimiter(...))` (`:48-56`) with `Window`, `PermitLimit`, `QueueLimit = 0` and `QueueProcessingOrder.OldestFirst` (`:51-54`). With no queue, a request over the limit is rejected at once (doc `:18-19`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the limiter itself is an internal type linked into several packages, so a public static factory is the one supported way for a consumer to reach it without widening the public API to the limiter class. Returning a `RateLimitPartition<TKey>` lets the result drop straight into a `PartitionedRateLimiter.Create` or `AddPolicy` selector (`:25`).
- **Where it's used**: no caller in `MMCA.Common/Source` or `MMCA.ADC/Source`; the framework's own partitions build the same limiter directly inside `CreateLimitedPartition` (`WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.RateLimiting.cs:312`). It is exercised by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/RateLimiting/RateLimitPartitionsTests.cs`. Consumer callers outside those two trees: not determinable from source read for this unit.

### StronglyTypedIdSchemaTransformer
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.OpenApi` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/OpenApi/StronglyTypedIdSchemaTransformer.cs:24` · Level 3 · class (sealed)

- **What it is**: an `IOpenApiSchemaTransformer` that rewrites the OpenAPI schema for a strongly typed identifier (ADR-115) so the generated document describes it as the primitive it wraps (an integer or a string), not as the wrapper's own object shape.
- **Depends on**: `Microsoft.AspNetCore.OpenApi.IOpenApiSchemaTransformer`, `Microsoft.OpenApi.Models.OpenApiSchema` and `JsonSchemaType`; the shared `StronglyTypedId.TryDescribe` helper that both this type and [StronglyTypedIdParameterTransformer](#stronglytypedidparametertransformer) call to recognize a wrapper and extract its underlying value type.
- **Concept introduced: OpenAPI schema transformers as the escape hatch for a type the JSON pipeline flattens but the schema generator does not.** `AddStronglyTypedIds` teaches `System.Text.Json` to serialize a wrapper as its bare value, but ASP.NET Core's OpenAPI generator still reflects the wrapper's own properties unless something intervenes; `IOpenApiSchemaTransformer` is exactly that intervention point, run once per discovered schema. `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]` assesses whether the published contract matches the wire format: without this transformer a client generator would model `OrderId` as `{ "value": 123 }` instead of a bare integer, which is not what the JSON converter actually emits.
- **Walkthrough**: `TransformAsync(OpenApiSchema, OpenApiSchemaTransformerContext, CancellationToken)` (`StronglyTypedIdSchemaTransformer.cs:28-45`):
  - Null-guards `schema` and `context` (`:33-34`).
  - `StronglyTypedId.TryDescribe(context.JsonTypeInfo.Type, out _, out var valueType)` (`:36`): a no-op return when the schema being generated is not for a strongly typed identifier (`:37`).
  - For a match, it clears `schema.Properties` and `schema.Required` (`:39-40`), which erases the object shape the reflection-based generator would otherwise have built, then sets `schema.Type = MapType(valueType)` and `schema.Format = MapFormat(valueType)` (`:41-42`).
  - `MapType` (`:47-50`, `internal static`) returns `JsonSchemaType.Integer` for `int`/`long`, else `JsonSchemaType.String`. `MapFormat` (`:52-61`) returns `"int32"`, `"int64"`, `"uuid"` (for `Guid`), or `null`, matching the OpenAPI format vocabulary a client generator already understands.
- **Why it's built this way**: both mapping methods are `internal static` rather than private, so [StronglyTypedIdParameterTransformer](#stronglytypedidparametertransformer) reuses the identical type/format decision for route and query parameters instead of duplicating the switch; the two transformers can never disagree about how one wrapper renders.
- **Where it's used**: registered unconditionally inside `AddCommonOpenApi()` via `options.AddSchemaTransformer(new StronglyTypedIdSchemaTransformer())` on every `OpenApiOptions` instance (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.cs:117`, inside the `ConfigureAll<OpenApiOptions>` block at `:502-506`, whose comment states it is inert in a host that declares no wrappers, `:498-501`). `ConfigureAll` rather than a single named document is deliberate: `AddOpenApi()` creates one `OpenApiOptions` per discovered API version, and the wire shape is identical in every one.

### StronglyTypedIdParameterTransformer
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.OpenApi` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/OpenApi/StronglyTypedIdSchemaTransformer.cs:74` · Level 4 · class (sealed)

- **What it is**: an `IOpenApiOperationTransformer` that rewrites the per-operation parameter schema for a strongly typed identifier bound from a route segment or a query string, the operation-level counterpart to [StronglyTypedIdSchemaTransformer](#stronglytypedidschematransformer).
- **Depends on**: `Microsoft.AspNetCore.OpenApi.IOpenApiOperationTransformer`, `Microsoft.OpenApi.Models.OpenApiParameter`/`OpenApiSchema`; [StronglyTypedIdSchemaTransformer](#stronglytypedidschematransformer)`.MapType`/`.MapFormat` for the type/format decision, and `StronglyTypedId.TryDescribe` to recognize a wrapper.
- **Concept**: a component-level schema and a parameter-level schema are generated by two different ASP.NET Core OpenAPI extension points, so fixing the component schema alone leaves an operation's own parameter list still describing the wrapper's object shape; the framework installs one transformer of each kind so both descriptions agree. `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]` applies for the same reason as the sibling type: a client generator reading only the operation's parameters (the common case for a route or query parameter) needs the primitive shape too.
- **Walkthrough**: `TransformAsync(OpenApiOperation, OpenApiOperationTransformerContext, CancellationToken)` (`StronglyTypedIdSchemaTransformer.cs:77-109`):
  - Null-guards `operation` and `context` (`:82-83`), then exits early when `operation.Parameters is null` (`:85-86`).
  - Iterates `context.Description.ParameterDescriptions` (`:88`), one entry per bound parameter on the action. For each, it resolves the effective type as `described.ModelMetadata?.ModelType ?? described.Type` (`:90`) and skips it when that type is null or is not a strongly typed identifier per `StronglyTypedId.TryDescribe` (`:91-92`).
  - Matches the description back to the operation's `OpenApiParameter` by name: `operation.Parameters.OfType<OpenApiParameter>().FirstOrDefault(p => string.Equals(p.Name, described.Name, StringComparison.Ordinal))` (`:94-96`), and skips a miss (`:98-99`).
  - Replaces `parameter.Schema` outright with a new `OpenApiSchema { Type = StronglyTypedIdSchemaTransformer.MapType(valueType), Format = StronglyTypedIdSchemaTransformer.MapFormat(valueType) }` (`:101-105`), rather than mutating the existing schema in place, since the wrapper's original parameter schema carries nothing worth preserving.
- **Why it's built this way**: reusing the sibling transformer's `MapType`/`MapFormat` instead of re-deriving the mapping keeps the component schema and every parameter schema for the same wrapper type identical by construction; there is no separate table to fall out of sync.
- **Where it's used**: registered unconditionally alongside its sibling inside `AddCommonOpenApi()` via `options.AddOperationTransformer(new StronglyTypedIdParameterTransformer())` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.cs:118`), inside the same `ConfigureAll<OpenApiOptions>` block (`:502-506`) that installs [StronglyTypedIdSchemaTransformer](#stronglytypedidschematransformer).

### CommonForwardedHeaders
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Startup` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/CommonForwardedHeaders.cs:27` · Level 0 · class (static)

- **What it is**: a two-member helper that names the framework's default forwarded-header mask and builds a fresh `ForwardedHeadersOptions` with that mask and a cleared proxy/network allowlist. It is the shared posture behind ASP.NET Core's own `UseForwardedHeaders` middleware.
- **Depends on**: `Microsoft.AspNetCore.HttpOverrides.ForwardedHeaders` and `ForwardedHeadersOptions` only. [CommonForwardedHeadersExtensions](#commonforwardedheadersextensions) and [MiddlewarePipelineBuilder](#middlewarepipelinebuilder) both call `Create`.
- **Concept (one named default, cleared allowlists by default).** `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]`: forwarded-header handling decides what scheme, host and client address every downstream component sees, so naming the default mask once (`X-Forwarded-For`, `X-Forwarded-Proto`, `X-Forwarded-Host`, `CommonForwardedHeaders.cs:31-32`) keeps every host consistent instead of re-typing the three-flag combination. Clearing `KnownProxies` and `KnownIPNetworks` rather than leaving ASP.NET Core's own defaults in place is deliberate: those lists exist to restrict which upstream hop is trusted, and a containerized host behind Aspire/DCP or an Azure ingress has no fixed proxy IP to allowlist, so the framework accepts the forwarded values from any hop and relies on the ingress topology itself to keep the header trustworthy.
- **Walkthrough**
  - `DefaultHeaders` (`:31-32`) is a static get-only property returning the three-flag `ForwardedHeaders` mask.
  - `Create(ForwardedHeaders? headers = null)` (`:40`) builds a `ForwardedHeadersOptions` with `ForwardedHeaders = headers ?? DefaultHeaders` (`:42-45`), clears both allowlists (`:47-48`), and returns the new instance (`:50`).
- **Why it's built this way**: returning a fresh options instance rather than a cached singleton means a caller that mutates the result (adding back a known proxy, say) never affects another caller's options.
- **Where it's used**: [MiddlewarePipelineBuilder](#middlewarepipelinebuilder) calls `CommonForwardedHeaders.Create()` for the default pipeline's forwarded-headers step (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/Pipeline/MiddlewarePipelineBuilder.cs:68`), and [CommonForwardedHeadersExtensions](#commonforwardedheadersextensions) calls it from the UI-host convenience wrapper. Both ADR-079 (shared HTTP middleware pipeline) and ADR-124 (Blazor circuit ceiling and UI edge) discuss forwarded-header handling at the edge.
