- **Caveats / not-in-source**: which strategy a given deployment runs is configuration, not code. The framework default is `"Migrate"` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Settings/ApplicationSettings.cs:43`); what any environment overrides it to lives in that app's settings and cannot be read here. The tenant pass is also only as complete as `TenancySettings`: `Expand` emits a tenant target only when the tenant declares an override connection string for that source, so a tenant that shares the default database is initialized by the shared pass and never appears in the per-tenant loop.

### AppAssociationOptions
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Startup.Endpoints` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/Endpoints/AppAssociationOptions.cs:9` · Level 0 · class (sealed)

- **What it is**: the strongly-typed options bag carrying the identifiers a mobile OS needs in order to verify that an installed native app may claim this host's https links. It feeds [AppAssociationEndpointExtensions](#appassociationendpointextensions), which serializes it into the two well-known documents (Android Digital Asset Links and the Apple App Site Association).
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party. BCL only (`IReadOnlyList<string>`).
- **Concept introduced (deep-link / universal-link association).** For a Blazor Hybrid app to open a shared web URL directly in the installed native app rather than the browser, the operating system fetches an association document from the URL's host and checks that the installed app's signing identity matches what the document names. This options type is the single source of those identities, so a certificate rotation is a config change and not a code change (the doc comment says exactly that at `AppAssociationOptions.cs:3-8`). `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]` assesses whether public contracts are typed and pinned rather than hand-built inline; here the "public contract" is the exact JSON payload Google and Apple parse, and binding its inputs from an `AppAssociation` configuration section is that discipline. `[Rubric §11, Security]` also applies: the fingerprints in this bag are what stop an unrelated app from claiming the host's links.
- **Walkthrough**: four members, all `init`-only.
  - `AndroidPackageName` (`AppAssociationOptions.cs:12`, `required`): the Android application id declared in `assetlinks.json`.
  - `AndroidCertFingerprints` (`AppAssociationOptions.cs:18`, defaults to `[]`): the SHA-256 signing-certificate fingerprints; the doc comment (`:14-17`) warns that for Play-distributed builds this is the Play App Signing certificate, not the local upload keystore.
  - `AppleAppId` (`AppAssociationOptions.cs:21`, `required`): the `TeamID.BundleID` value used by both the `webcredentials` and the `applinks` sections.
  - `AppleAppLinkComponents` (`AppAssociationOptions.cs:28`, defaults to `[]`): the URL patterns (for example `"/conference/*"`) that each become a `{ "/": pattern }` component; the comment (`:23-27`) notes these should mirror the app's shared Blazor routes, because identical URLs on web and device is the Blazor Hybrid payoff (no route-translation table).
- **Why it's built this way**: `required init` gives compile-checked construction plus immutability once bound (see the primer on [required/init immutability](00-primer.md#2-architectural-styles-this-codebase-commits-to)), which matches the lifetime: a host builds one instance at startup and the endpoint reads it for the process lifetime. Defaulting the two collections to `[]` means a host that ships only one platform still constructs a valid document for the other. See [ADR-043](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/043-mobile-deep-links-and-native-oauth-callback.html) for the deep-link decision this serves.
- **Where it's used**: constructed inline by the ADC Blazor web host and passed straight to the mapper, with the Android and Apple identifiers read from the `AppAssociation` configuration section (with in-code fallbacks) and the applinks patterns hard-coded to the app's routes (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/UI/MMCA.ADC.UI.Web/Program.cs:143-154`). The comment there (`:180-183`) records a trap worth reading: the Release Android head overrides `ApplicationId` to `ivanball.AtlDevCon`, so that is the package Digital Asset Links must name, not the Debug-only id.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the type performs no validation. Whether a fingerprint or bundle id is the *correct* one for the shipped app is only observable at install time on the device.

### FeatureFlagOverrideSettings
> MMCA.Common.Application · `MMCA.Common.Application.FeatureFlags` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/FeatureFlags/FeatureFlagOverrideSettings.cs:9` · Level 0 · class (sealed, options)

- **What it is**: the options bag for the reloadable feature flags (stored overrides, [ADR-031](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/031-feature-flag-management.html)), bound from the `FeatureFlags` configuration section by `AddReloadableFeatureFlags` (doc comment `FeatureFlagOverrideSettings.cs:5-8`).
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party. `System.ComponentModel.DataAnnotations` for `[Range]` and `[Required]`.
- **Concept (a bounded cache lifetime as a stated staleness contract).** `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]` assesses whether an operator can predict how a runtime change propagates; the remarks on `OverrideCacheSeconds` (`FeatureFlagOverrideSettings.cs:17-22`) state that a write invalidates the snapshot at once on the replica that served it, and that this value is the upper bound on how long another replica keeps serving the old snapshot when there is no distributed L2 and shared Redis for the generation tokens. `[Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]` also applies: both members carry DataAnnotations and the registration validates them on start, which is the fail-fast configuration contract of [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html).
- **Walkthrough**
  - `SectionName` (`FeatureFlagOverrideSettings.cs:12`) is the constant `"FeatureFlags"`.
  - `OverrideCacheSeconds` (`:24`, default `30`) is how long the cached override snapshot is served before it is reloaded, constrained to `[Range(1, 3600)]` (`:23`).
  - `DataSourceName` (`:31`, default `"Default"`, `[Required]` at `:30`) names the logical data source that owns the override table: the owning service's own database. The default suits a single-database host (`:26-29`).
- **Why it's built this way**: `init`-only properties with defaults mean a host that sets nothing still gets a valid 30-second, single-database configuration, while a bad value (zero seconds, a blank source) fails at startup rather than on the first flag read. `AddReloadableFeatureFlags` binds it with `.ValidateDataAnnotations().ValidateOnStart()` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.FeatureFlags.cs:64-67`).
- **Where it's used**: the shipped `FeatureFlagOverrideSnapshot` turns `OverrideCacheSeconds` into its cache lifetime and folds `DataSourceName` into its cache-key prefix (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/FeatureFlags/FeatureFlagOverrideSnapshot.cs:55`, `:62`); `ApplicationDbContext` reads `DataSourceName` to decide which database instance maps the override table (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/DbContexts/ApplicationDbContext.cs:1093`). The ADC Conference and Engagement services opt in through `AddReloadableFeatureFlags` (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:309`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Service/Program.cs:130`). See [IFeatureFlagOverrideSnapshot](#ifeatureflagoverridesnapshot).

### MiddlewarePipelineStep
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Startup.Pipeline` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/Pipeline/MiddlewarePipelineStep.cs:21` · Level 0 · record (sealed, positional)

- **What it is**: one named step of the shared HTTP edge pipeline: a stable string identifier plus the delegate that registers that step's middleware on a `WebApplication`. It is the atom that makes the edge order *data* rather than a hand-written sequence of `app.UseX()` calls.
- **Depends on**: ASP.NET Core's `WebApplication` (through the `Action<WebApplication>` payload) and nothing else first-party. Its names normally come from [MiddlewarePipelineStepNames](#middlewarepipelinestepnames); its container is [MiddlewarePipelineBuilder](#middlewarepipelinebuilder).
- **Concept introduced (the pipeline as an inspectable list, not an imperative script).** A conventional ASP.NET composition root *is* its own ordering: the order exists only as the sequence of statements in `Program.cs`, so nothing can read it, assert on it, or edit it. Modelling each step as a value with a name means the whole order can be enumerated (`StepNames` on the builder), rewritten by name, validated, and frozen by a unit test with no host running at all. The doc comment states the payoff directly: steps are pure data until `UseCommonMiddlewarePipeline` runs them in order, which is what makes the pipeline order testable without a running host (`MiddlewarePipelineStep.cs:5-10`). `[Rubric §2, Design Patterns]` assesses whether a recognizable pattern is applied where it earns its keep; this is the classic "reify the plan, then execute it" split, and it is what unlocks the fitness function. `[Rubric §14, Testability]` assesses whether behavior can be asserted cheaply: because a step never touches a host until `Configure` is invoked, the entire order runs in the fast unit tier.
- **Walkthrough**: a positional record with two components, each re-declared as a validated property.
  - `Name` (`MiddlewarePipelineStep.cs:27`) shadows the positional parameter with `Validated(Name)`, which calls `ArgumentException.ThrowIfNullOrWhiteSpace` (`:32-36`). Null, empty, and whitespace names are rejected at construction, so an anchor lookup can never match a meaningless key.
  - `Configure` (`:30`) is validated the same way through the second `Validated` overload, which null-guards the delegate (`:38-42`).
  - The `init` accessors keep both immutable after construction, so a step handed to the builder cannot be mutated behind the builder's back.
  - The parameter doc records the runtime contract: `Configure` is invoked exactly once, in pipeline order, at the point `UseCommonMiddlewarePipeline` is called, so anything the delegate reads from the host (configuration, environment) is evaluated at configure time and not per request (`:16-20`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the validation-in-the-property-initializer idiom is how a positional record enforces invariants without giving up the concise declaration or the value semantics. Value equality also matters here: two steps with the same name and delegate compare equal, which keeps assertions in [MiddlewarePipelineBuilder](#middlewarepipelinebuilder) tests simple. See [ADR-079](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/079-shared-http-middleware-pipeline.html), which records the move from an inline sequence to named steps.
- **Where it's used**: [MiddlewarePipelineBuilder](#middlewarepipelinebuilder)`.CreateDefault()` constructs eighteen of them (`MiddlewarePipelineBuilder.cs:31-159`), and a host customizing the pipeline constructs its own to pass to `InsertBefore` / `InsertAfter` / `Replace`.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: name uniqueness is *not* enforced here; it is enforced by the builder's `RequireUniqueName` at insertion time (`MiddlewarePipelineBuilder.cs:307-315`). Constructing two steps with the same name in isolation is legal.

### MiddlewarePipelineStepNames
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Startup.Pipeline` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/Pipeline/MiddlewarePipelineStepNames.cs:14` · Level 0 · class (static, constants)

- **What it is**: the nineteen well-known step names of the default edge pipeline, as `const string` fields declared in runtime order. A host customizing the pipeline addresses steps by these constants.
- **Depends on**: nothing. It is referenced by [MiddlewarePipelineBuilder](#middlewarepipelinebuilder) (which seeds the defaults with these names and re-checks adjacencies by them) and by `MiddlewarePipelineOrderTestsBase`.
- **Concept (names as a published contract).** Because a host inserts, replaces, and removes steps *by name*, the names are part of the framework's public API surface: the doc comment says so outright and adds that renaming one is a breaking change (`MiddlewarePipelineStepNames.cs:3-7`). `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]` assesses whether the surface a consumer binds to is explicit and stable; hoisting each name into a `const` is what turns a magic string into that surface, and what lets the compiler find every caller when the list changes. `[Rubric §34, Architecture Governance & Documentation]` also applies: the declaration order below the summary *is* the documented runtime order, so the code and the documentation cannot drift apart.
- **Walkthrough**: the constants in declaration order, which is application order (outermost first).
  - `ExceptionHandler` (`:17`), `CorrelationId` (`:20`), `RequestLocalization` (`:23`).
  - `PreForwardedCapture` (`:29`) and `ForwardedHeaders` (`:32`). The comment on the first (`:25-28`) states the adjacency: it must run immediately before `ForwardedHeaders`, because it captures the transport scheme and host as the connection saw them, before the forwarded headers rewrite them.
  - `HttpsRedirection` (`:35`), `ResponseCompression` (`:38`), `Routing` (`:41`), `Cors` (`:44`), `Authentication` (`:47`).
  - `TenantResolution` (`:53`), whose comment (`:49-52`) records that it must run immediately after `Authentication` because the claim strategy reads `HttpContext.User`.
  - `RateLimiting` (`:56`), documented as needing to run after `Authentication` per [ADR-019](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/019-rate-limiting.html).
  - `SoftDeletedUserFilter` (`:59`).
  - `ViewAsGuard` (`:65`), the read-only view-as guard of [ADR-137](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/137-read-only-view-as-support-access.html) wired as [ViewAsGuardMiddleware](#viewasguardmiddleware). Its comment (`:61-64`) records that the step is always present: with `ViewAs:Enabled` false it refuses every view-as token.
  - `Authorization` (`:68`), `OutputCache` (`:71`).
  - `JwksEndpoint` (`:74`), `OidcDiscoveryEndpoint` (`:77`), and `Controllers` (`:80`), the innermost step.
  - The class summary (`:8-12`) flags that several of these adjacencies are load-bearing and are re-checked by [MiddlewarePipelineBuilder](#middlewarepipelinebuilder)`.Build`.
- **Why it's built this way**: `const` rather than `static readonly` so the values are usable in attribute arguments and `switch` patterns, and one file rather than a nested enum so the XML doc on each field can carry the ordering rationale next to the name it explains. See [ADR-079](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/079-shared-http-middleware-pipeline.html).
- **Where it's used**: [MiddlewarePipelineBuilder](#middlewarepipelinebuilder)`.CreateDefault()` names every seeded step with these constants; `Build()` names them again in its four invariant checks; and `MiddlewarePipelineOrderTestsBase.ExpectedStepNames` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Testing/Conformance/MiddlewarePipelineOrderTestsBase.cs:40-61`) lists all nineteen as the frozen expected order, which each app's `MiddlewarePipelineOrderTests` subclasses.

### OpenApiEndpointExtensions
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Startup.Endpoints` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/Endpoints/OpenApiEndpointExtensions.cs:24` · Level 0 · class (static, extension block)

- **What it is**: one `extension(WebApplication app)` mapping helper, `MapCommonOpenApi()`, that exposes the generated OpenAPI document **outside Production only**.
- **Depends on**: `Microsoft.AspNetCore.OpenApi` for `MapOpenApi()` and the keyed `IOpenApiDocumentProvider` it probes. It pairs with the host's own `services.AddOpenApi()`, which registers the `v1` document, plus `AddCommonOpenApi()` on [WebApplicationBuilderExtensions](#webapplicationbuilderextensions), which only configures the registered documents and registers none of its own (doc comment `OpenApiEndpointExtensions.cs:12-13`, `:37-38`).
- **Concept introduced (the OpenAPI document as a dev/CI artifact, not a public surface).** `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]` assesses whether an API has a machine-readable contract and whether that contract is guarded against silent drift. The doc comment (`OpenApiEndpointExtensions.cs:9-22`) is explicit that the guarding happens at **two levels**: the framework-owned part of the generated document (the unbound-route-token backfill, the generated `ProblemDetails` error schema) is diffed against a committed baseline in-repo by `OpenApiBaselineTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/OpenApi/OpenApiBaselineTests.cs`), which fails on any change until the baseline is regenerated deliberately in the same pull request, while each consumer's concrete API surface stays the concern of that host's committed document and its contract-snapshot tests. Mapping outside Production is the security posture `[Rubric §11, Security]`: these are internal services reached through the Gateway, which does not route the endpoint.
- **Walkthrough**
  - `DefaultDocumentName` (`OpenApiEndpointExtensions.cs:27`) is the private constant `"v1"`: the document name plain `AddOpenApi()` registers and this endpoint serves.
  - `MapCommonOpenApi()` (`OpenApiEndpointExtensions.cs:59`) does nothing in Production and returns `app` for chaining (`:76`). Outside Production (`:61`) it first resolves `IServiceProviderIsKeyedService` and checks that a keyed `IOpenApiDocumentProvider` exists for `"v1"` (`:63-64`); if not, it throws `InvalidOperationException` telling the host to call `services.AddOpenApi()` in the host project itself, because that is the call the OpenAPI XML-comment generator intercepts to attach the host's summaries (`:66-70`). A host that calls only the framework method therefore fails at startup (and at build-time document generation) instead of silently serving no document (doc comment `:36-40`). It then maps the document with `app.MapOpenApi().AllowAnonymous()` (`:73`). The anonymous declaration is load-bearing: the framework's fallback authorization policy gates every endpoint that states nothing and `/openapi` is not one of its exempt prefixes, so an undeclared mapping would answer 401 to the contract-snapshot tests and API tooling (doc comment `:42-49`).
  - The method carries a `[SuppressMessage("Usage", "AV0030:Missing WithDocumentPerVersion")]` (`:55-58`). The justification records why: the analyzer fires because `AddCommonApiVersioning`'s `AddApiExplorer` call lives in this assembly, but no versioned OpenAPI registration exists (`Asp.Versioning.OpenApi` is not referenced, so `WithDocumentPerVersion()` is not available). The host serves the single plain `v1` document.
  - There is no interactive reference UI in the framework: the class exposes only the JSON document.
- **Why it's built this way**: one shared helper keeps every service's OpenAPI story identical and enforces the "internal spec, not public surface" convention in one place instead of per host. The registration guard turns a missing host `AddOpenApi()` call (which would otherwise lose the XML-comment summaries or serve nothing) into a startup failure, and the explicit `AllowAnonymous()` keeps the contract reachable before a client holds a token without widening the fallback policy's exempt list. API versioning itself is [ADR-046](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/046-http-api-versioning.html).
- **Where it's used**: service hosts reach it through the shared service-host composition, which calls `app.MapCommonOpenApi()` right after `UseCommonMiddlewarePipeline` ([ServiceHostApplicationExtensions](#servicehostapplicationextensions), `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.ServiceHost/ServiceHostApplicationExtensions.cs:66`). The ADC integration tiers subclass `OpenApiContractTestsBase` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Testing/Conformance/OpenApiContractTestsBase.cs:32`), for example `MMCA.ADC/Tests/Integration/MMCA.ADC.Conference.IntegrationTests/Contract/OpenApiContractTests.cs:15`. In the framework, `OpenApiProbeHost` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/OpenApi/OpenApiProbeHost.cs:44-45` registers, `:65` maps) is what `OpenApiBaselineTests` boots, and `MapCommonOpenApiAuthorizationTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/OpenApi/MapCommonOpenApiAuthorizationTests.cs:23`) pins the anonymous declaration and the Production no-op.

### OversizeFormResourceFilter
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Uploads` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Uploads/OversizeFormResourceFilter.cs:22` · Level 0 · class (internal sealed, MVC resource filter)

- **What it is**: a global `IAsyncResourceFilter` that reads the request form before model binding for any action that binds a form or a form file, so a body over the endpoint's size limit answers 413 Payload Too Large instead of 400 (doc comment `OversizeFormResourceFilter.cs:8-14`).
- **Depends on**: ASP.NET Core MVC filter and model-binding abstractions (`IAsyncResourceFilter`, `ActionDescriptor`, `BindingSource`) and `BadHttpRequestException`. Its 413 reaches [GlobalExceptionHandler](#globalexceptionhandler), which answers it as "Payload Too Large".
- **Concept (the right status code for a refused body).** `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]` assesses whether an API reports failures with accurate, consistent status codes. Without this filter, model binding catches the server's "request body too large" refusal and turns it into a model-state error, which `[ApiController]` answers as 400: a client cannot tell "your form is malformed" from "your file is too big". Reading the form one step earlier lets the same refusal surface as a `BadHttpRequestException` carrying 413 (`:8-14`).
- **Walkthrough**
  - `FilterOrder` (`OversizeFormResourceFilter.cs:29`) is `1000`: after `[RequestSizeLimit]` and `[RequestFormLimits]` (both order 900), so the endpoint's own limits are applied before the body is read (`:24-28`).
  - `OnResourceExecutionAsync` (`:32`) null-guards both arguments (`:34-35`), then only when the request has a form content type **and** the action binds a form (`:38`) calls `ReadFormAsync` with the request-aborted token (`:42`). A `BadHttpRequestException` whose status is not 413 is swallowed (`:44`), as is `InvalidDataException` (`:48`), so a malformed form or one over a form limit is still reported by model binding as before. A 413 propagates. It always calls `next()` (`:54`).
  - `BindsForm` (`:57-60`) is true when any action parameter's binding source is `Form` or `FormFile`.
- **Why it's built this way**: the change is narrow on purpose (`:15-20`). Only the oversize refusal changes; an action that binds no form (a streaming upload that reads the body itself) is never touched, so its body is not buffered here; and the form read is cached on the request, so binding does not read the body twice.
- **Where it's used**: registered globally by `AddAPI` on [DependencyInjection](#dependencyinjection) as `options.Filters.Add<OversizeFormResourceFilter>(OversizeFormResourceFilter.FilterOrder)` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/DependencyInjection.cs:53`), so every MVC host gets it.

### UploadedFileContent
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Uploads` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Uploads/FormFileUploadExtensions.cs:16` · Level 0 · record (sealed, positional)

- **What it is**: an uploaded file read into memory by `ReadBoundedAsync`: the bytes, plus the file name and content type exactly as the client declared them (doc comment `FormFileUploadExtensions.cs:8-14`).
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party.
- **Concept**: the result shape of the bounded upload read taught on [FormFileUploadExtensions](#formfileuploadextensions).
- **Walkthrough**: `Content` (`byte[]`), `FileName` (not normalized) and `ContentType` (`:16`). The class-level `[SuppressMessage("Performance", "CA1819")]` (`:15`) records why the record exposes an array: the bytes go to a command unchanged, and a defensive copy would double the upload in memory.
- **Why it's built this way**: the declared name and type travel beside the bytes untouched, so any normalization or content sniffing is the receiving command's decision, not the edge's. `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]`: one buffer of the declared length, never copied again.
- **Where it's used**: returned by `ReadBoundedAsync` and carried inside [CsvUpload](#csvupload); ADC upload actions consume it (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Conference/MMCA.ADC.Conference.API/Controllers/SessionAssets/SessionAssetsController.cs:197`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Identity/MMCA.ADC.Identity.API/Controllers/UsersController.cs:171`). Covered by `FormFileUploadExtensionsTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/Uploads/FormFileUploadExtensionsTests.cs`).

### AppAssociationEndpointExtensions
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Startup.Endpoints` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/Endpoints/AppAssociationEndpointExtensions.cs:15` · Level 1 · class (static, extension block)

- **What it is**: a mapping helper that serves the two well-known app-association documents from an [AppAssociationOptions](#appassociationoptions): Android Digital Asset Links at `/.well-known/assetlinks.json` and the Apple App Site Association at `/.well-known/apple-app-site-association`.
- **Depends on**: [AppAssociationOptions](#appassociationoptions) (Level 0) for every value; ASP.NET `IEndpointRouteBuilder` and `Results.Json`.
- **Concept (anonymous, machine-verified association documents).** Both endpoints are anonymous by design because the OS and Apple's CDN fetch them without credentials, which the doc comment states (`AppAssociationEndpointExtensions.cs:12-13`). `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]`: the exact JSON shape is a contract a third party parses, so the code builds it structurally out of dictionaries rather than formatting strings by hand.
- **Walkthrough**
  - Two path constants: `AssetLinksPath` (`AppAssociationEndpointExtensions.cs:18`) and `AppleAppSiteAssociationPath` (`:24`). The comment on the Apple constant (`:20-23`) records that the path deliberately has no file extension because Apple requires that exact path, while the content type must still be JSON.
  - `MapAppAssociationEndpoints(AppAssociationOptions options)` (`:35`) null-guards the options (`:37`), builds both documents once at map time because they are static for the process lifetime (`:39-40`), then maps two `GET`s that each return `Results.Json(...)`, are `.AllowAnonymous()` and are `.ExcludeFromDescription()` so they never leak into the OpenAPI document (`:42-48`).
  - `BuildAssetLinks` (`:54`) emits the `delegate_permission/common.handle_all_urls` relation with the Android package name and the fingerprint list (`:56-65`).
  - `BuildAppleAppSiteAssociation` (`:68`) emits the `applinks` details block, projecting each configured URL pattern into a `{ "/": pattern }` component (`:78-80`), plus the `webcredentials` apps list naming the same app id (`:84-87`).
- **Why it's built this way**: building the payload once at map time avoids a per-request allocation for a document that never changes `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]`, and holding the RFC 8615 well-known paths as public constants keeps them from drifting between hosts or between the endpoint and any gateway forwarding rule.
- **Where it's used**: the ADC Blazor web host maps them once at startup (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/UI/MMCA.ADC.UI.Web/Program.cs:144`), which is the host that ships a companion MAUI Hybrid app. Both documents' exact shapes are asserted by `AppAssociationEndpointTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/Startup/AppAssociationEndpointTests.cs`).

### JwksEndpointExtensions
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Startup.Endpoints` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/Endpoints/JwksEndpointExtensions.cs:15` · Level 1 · class (static, extension block)

- **What it is**: maps `/.well-known/jwks.json`, serializing the active `JsonWebKeySet` of the Identity service so other services can validate its RS256 tokens.
- **Depends on**: [IJwksProvider](group-08-auth.md#ijwksprovider), resolved from DI per request, whose implementation is [RsaJwksProvider](group-08-auth.md#rsajwksprovider); plus `Microsoft.IdentityModel.Tokens.JsonWebKeySet` and `System.Text.Json`.
- **Concept (the public-key distribution endpoint of cross-service auth).** `[Rubric §11, Security]` and `[Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]`: with RS256 only the Identity service holds the private key, and every other service fetches the public keys from here, so no shared secret ever crosses a service boundary ([ADR-004](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/004-authentication-dual-fetch.html)). The endpoint is `.AllowAnonymous()` (`JwksEndpointExtensions.cs:39`) because clients fetch it *before* they have a token, which is what JWKS means (RFC 7517; the doc comment says so at `:27-28`).
- **Walkthrough**: the `DefaultJwksPath` constant (`JwksEndpointExtensions.cs:20`) pins the RFC 8615 path. `MapJwksEndpoint()` (`:31`) maps a single `GET` whose handler takes `HttpContext` and `IJwksProvider` as parameters (`:33`), calls `GetJsonWebKeySet()` (`:35`), serializes with `JsonSerializer` (`:36`), sets `application/json; charset=utf-8` explicitly (`:37`), and writes the body (`:38`). The whole endpoint is under ten lines because the key material and its rotation live behind the provider.
- **Why it's built this way**: non-Identity hosts still map it (the `JwksEndpoint` step is unconditional in the default pipeline, `MiddlewarePipelineBuilder.cs:143-150`); their provider returns an empty key set rather than erroring, so the wiring is uniform across every host and a single gateway forwarder rule for `/.well-known/*` covers JWKS discovery for the whole platform. That same prefix is one of the paths the global rate limiter bypasses (`WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.RateLimiting.cs:79`).
- **Where it's used**: applied as the `JwksEndpoint` step of the default pipeline seeded by [MiddlewarePipelineBuilder](#middlewarepipelinebuilder) (`MiddlewarePipelineBuilder.cs:150`), so every host that adopts the shared pipeline serves it; the path it owns is the `jwks_uri` value that [OidcDiscoveryEndpointExtensions](#oidcdiscoveryendpointextensions) advertises, which is in turn what `AddForwardedJwtBearer` (on [WebApplicationBuilderExtensions](#webapplicationbuilderextensions)) reaches through OIDC discovery. Its behavior is covered by `JwksEndpointTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/Startup/JwksEndpointTests.cs`).

### CsvUpload
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Uploads` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Uploads/FormFileUploadExtensions.cs:24` · Level 2 · record (sealed, positional)

- **What it is**: the result of `ReadCsvAsync`: the raw upload beside the parsed table, so one read serves both the edge's own validation and dry run and a command that stores or re-parses the file (doc comment `FormFileUploadExtensions.cs:18-23`).
- **Depends on**: [UploadedFileContent](#uploadedfilecontent) (Level 0) for `File`, and [CsvTable](group-01-result-error-handling.md#csvtable) for `Table`.
- **Concept**: the CSV flavor of the bounded upload read taught on [FormFileUploadExtensions](#formfileuploadextensions).
- **Walkthrough**: two positional components (`:24`). `File` is the raw upload, bytes unchanged (`:22`); `Table` is the parsed table (`:23`).
- **Why it's built this way**: keeping the bytes next to the parsed rows means an action never has to choose between validating the table at the edge and handing the original file on: the CSV is read from the request once, and parsed once from the same buffer. `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]`: no second read of the request body and no second copy of the bytes.
- **Where it's used**: built only by `ReadCsvCoreAsync` on success (`FormFileUploadExtensions.cs:128`). The ADC ticket roster import consumes it (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Engagement/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.API/Controllers/TicketRosterImportController.cs:77`). Covered by `FormFileCsvUploadTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/Uploads/FormFileCsvUploadTests.cs`).

### IFeatureFlagOverrideSnapshot
> MMCA.Common.Application · `MMCA.Common.Application.FeatureFlags` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/FeatureFlags/IFeatureFlagOverrideSnapshot.cs:14` · Level 2 · interface

- **What it is**: the cached whole-table snapshot of the stored feature-flag overrides that every flag evaluation reads, plus the invalidation every write performs ([ADR-031](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/031-feature-flag-management.html); doc comment `IFeatureFlagOverrideSnapshot.cs:5-8`).
- **Depends on**: [FeatureFlagOverrideResponse](#featureflagoverrideresponse), the element type it returns.
- **Concept (one snapshot, not a lookup per flag).** A flag check sits on the request path, so loading every override in one query on a slow interval, and again right after an edit, keeps that check a cache read; the remarks borrow the reasoning of the permission-grant cache (`IFeatureFlagOverrideSnapshot.cs:9-13`). `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]` assesses whether hot-path reads avoid a database round trip; `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]` applies to the staleness bound that [FeatureFlagOverrideSettings](#featureflagoverridesettings)`.OverrideCacheSeconds` states.
- **Walkthrough**
  - `GetAsync` (`IFeatureFlagOverrideSnapshot.cs:19`) returns the active overrides, from the cache when it holds them.
  - `InvalidateAsync` (`:24`) drops the cached snapshot so the next read reloads it from the store.
- **Why it's built this way**: separating the cached read from the store ([IFeatureFlagOverrideStore](#ifeatureflagoverridestore)) keeps the evaluator off the database. The shipped implementation, the internal `FeatureFlagOverrideSnapshot`, holds the whole table as one entry in the host's `ICacheService` under a key that carries the host's application name and the data source name, and invalidates by prefix, so on a host with the two-level cache a write on one replica retires the snapshot on every replica (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/FeatureFlags/FeatureFlagOverrideSnapshot.cs:11-37`). It is a singleton that opens its own DI scope per load because the store is scoped (`:35-36`, `:104`).
- **Where it's used**: registered as a singleton by `AddReloadableFeatureFlags` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.FeatureFlags.cs:74`); read by [StoredOverrideFeatureDefinitionProvider](#storedoverridefeaturedefinitionprovider) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/FeatureFlags/StoredOverrideFeatureDefinitionProvider.cs:33`) and invalidated by the shipped [IFeatureFlagAdministrationService](#ifeatureflagadministrationservice) after each write (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/FeatureFlags/FeatureFlagAdministrationService.cs:33`). Fallback behavior is covered by `StoredOverrideFallbackTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Tests/FeatureFlags/StoredOverrideFallbackTests.cs`).

### OidcDiscoveryEndpointExtensions
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Startup.Endpoints` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/Endpoints/OidcDiscoveryEndpointExtensions.cs:22` · Level 2 · class (static, extension block)

- **What it is**: maps a minimal OpenID Connect discovery document at `/.well-known/openid-configuration`. It returns just enough for token validation (the `issuer` and `jwks_uri` fields, plus three supported-value arrays), so a downstream service that points its JWT authority here can discover the signing keys automatically. When no issuer is configured it returns `404`.
- **Depends on**: [JwksEndpointExtensions](#jwksendpointextensions)`.DefaultJwksPath` to compose the `jwks_uri` (`OidcDiscoveryEndpointExtensions.cs:72`); `IConfiguration` for `Jwt:Issuer`; `System.Text.Json`.
- **Concept (OIDC discovery as the bootstrap for JWKS-based validation).** `[Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]` and `[Rubric §11, Security]`: `AddForwardedJwtBearer` sets an `Authority`, and the JWT bearer middleware fetches `{authority}/.well-known/openid-configuration` to learn the issuer and the JWKS URL. This endpoint answers that fetch, which is the other half of [ADR-004](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/004-authentication-dual-fetch.html) alongside [JwksEndpointExtensions](#jwksendpointextensions).
- **Walkthrough**
  - `DefaultOidcDiscoveryPath` constant (`OidcDiscoveryEndpointExtensions.cs:27`).
  - Three static arrays (`token`, `public`, `RS256`, `:32-34`) and an `OidcJsonOptions` with `PropertyNamingPolicy = null` (`:43-46`). Disabling the naming policy is load-bearing: the field names are already OIDC snake_case per RFC 8414, and camelCasing `jwks_uri` to `jwksUri` would leave `OpenIdConnectConfigurationRetriever` unable to recognise the document (`:36-42`). The fields sit under a scoped `#pragma warning disable IDE0052` (`:31`, restored `:47`) because the analyzer does not look into C# extension blocks for field usage, and the comment records that (`:29-30`).
  - `MapOidcDiscoveryEndpoint()` (`:58`) maps a `GET` that is `.AllowAnonymous()` (`:86`) and reads `Jwt:Issuer` (`:62`); a blank issuer returns `Results.NotFound()` (`:63-66`), safe because no downstream points its authority at a non-Identity host. Otherwise it derives `jwks_uri` from the configured issuer rather than the inbound request (`:76`) and returns the issuer, that URI, and the three supported-value arrays (`:78-85`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the comment at `:68-75` documents the subtle reason `jwks_uri` is built from the configured issuer and not from the request. Aspire/DCP fronts the Identity service on per-launchSettings ports and rewrites `Host` via `X-Forwarded-Host` to canonical ports that internal callers cannot always reach, so reusing the issuer keeps issuer and `jwks_uri` origin-aligned (a common OIDC client requirement) and routes both through the same gateway that fronts `/Auth`, which means one forwarder rule for `/.well-known/*` covers everything.
- **Where it's used**: applied unconditionally as the `OidcDiscoveryEndpoint` step of the default pipeline (`MiddlewarePipelineBuilder.cs:152-154`); consumed by the bearer middleware that `AddForwardedJwtBearer` configures on [WebApplicationBuilderExtensions](#webapplicationbuilderextensions), which deliberately leaves `ValidIssuer` unset so the issuer comes from this document (`WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.Authentication.cs:90-103`). Covered by `OidcDiscoveryEndpointTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/Startup/OidcDiscoveryEndpointTests.cs`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the pre-forwarded scheme and host captured into `HttpContext.Items` by the `PreForwardedCapture` step exist for exactly this endpoint's benefit (`WebApplicationExtensions.cs:18-35`), but the current handler composes `jwks_uri` from the configured issuer only, so those items are not read on this path today.

### IFeatureFlagAdministrationService
> MMCA.Common.Application · `MMCA.Common.Application.FeatureFlags` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/FeatureFlags/IFeatureFlagAdministrationService.cs:17` · Level 3 · interface

- **What it is**: the administration surface of the reloadable feature flags ([ADR-031](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/031-feature-flag-management.html)): list the flags this service declares, and set or clear their stored overrides. Registered by `AddReloadableFeatureFlags` and served by [FeatureFlagsControllerBase](#featureflagscontrollerbase) (doc comment `IFeatureFlagAdministrationService.cs:6-10`).
- **Depends on**: [Result](group-01-result-error-handling.md#result); [FeatureFlagStatusResponse](#featureflagstatusresponse) and [SetFeatureFlagOverrideRequest](#setfeatureflagoverriderequest).
- **Concept (only a declared flag can be overridden).** The declared set is what [FeatureFlagRegistry](#featureflagregistry)`.Describe` reports over the assemblies the host passed to `AddReloadableFeatureFlags`, so an override cannot be written for a name no code reads: a typo is refused with `FeatureFlag.Undeclared` rather than stored and left silently inert (`IFeatureFlagAdministrationService.cs:11-16`). `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]`: failures come back as `Result` values naming the cause, not as exceptions. `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]`: an operator changes a flag at runtime through one audited surface.
- **Walkthrough**
  - `ListAsync` (`IFeatureFlagAdministrationService.cs:22`) lists every declared flag with its configured value, its override and its effective value, ordered by name.
  - `SetOverrideAsync(name, request)` (`:28`) stores the override of one declared flag and invalidates the cached snapshot, returning the flag's state afterwards or a failure (an undeclared name, an invalid override).
  - `ClearOverrideAsync(name)` (`:38`) clears the override and invalidates the snapshot; it fails only for an undeclared name.
- **Why it's built this way**: the declared-name check, the store write and the invalidation live in one service so a controller cannot skip a step. The shipped internal `FeatureFlagAdministrationService` also records that the invalidation never fails a committed write: a cache fault after the row is saved is logged and the call still succeeds, and the entry lifetime bounds how long the old snapshot can be served (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/FeatureFlags/FeatureFlagAdministrationService.cs:9-24`).
- **Where it's used**: registered scoped by `AddReloadableFeatureFlags` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.FeatureFlags.cs:75`); injected into [FeatureFlagsControllerBase](#featureflagscontrollerbase) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Controllers/Administration/FeatureFlagsControllerBase.cs:38`), which the ADC Conference and Engagement `AdminFeatureFlagsController` subclasses receive (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Conference/MMCA.ADC.Conference.API/Controllers/FeatureFlags/AdminFeatureFlagsController.cs:23`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Engagement/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.API/Controllers/AdminFeatureFlagsController.cs:23`). Covered by `FeatureFlagsControllerBaseTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/Controllers/Administration/FeatureFlagsControllerBaseTests.cs`).

### IFeatureFlagOverrideStore
> MMCA.Common.Application · `MMCA.Common.Application.FeatureFlags` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/FeatureFlags/IFeatureFlagOverrideStore.cs:15` · Level 3 · interface

- **What it is**: the persistence of stored feature-flag overrides ([ADR-031](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/031-feature-flag-management.html)). The shipped implementation is the EF store `AddReloadableFeatureFlags` registers over the owning service's own database (doc comment `IFeatureFlagOverrideStore.cs:6-9`).
- **Depends on**: [Result](group-01-result-error-handling.md#result); [FeatureFlagOverrideResponse](#featureflagoverrideresponse) and [SetFeatureFlagOverrideRequest](#setfeatureflagoverriderequest).
- **Concept (a dumb store under a smart service).** The store does not check that a name is a declared flag, and its writes do not invalidate the cached snapshot: the administration service does both, around each call (`IFeatureFlagOverrideStore.cs:10-14`). `[Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]`: each rule has exactly one owner, so the store stays a plain persistence boundary.
- **Walkthrough**
  - `GetAllAsync` (`IFeatureFlagOverrideStore.cs:20`) reads every active override, ordered by flag name; cleared ones are not included.
  - `SetAsync(name, request)` (`:26`) creates or replaces the override of one flag, returning the stored override or a validation failure.
  - `ClearAsync(name)` (`:36`) clears the override; clearing a flag with no override succeeds.
- **Why it's built this way**: the overrides live in the owning service's own database (database per service), selected by [FeatureFlagOverrideSettings](#featureflagoverridesettings)`.DataSourceName`; keeping the interface in Application lets a host substitute a store without touching the evaluator or the snapshot.
- **Where it's used**: registered scoped as the internal `EFFeatureFlagOverrideStore` by `AddReloadableFeatureFlags` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.FeatureFlags.cs:73`; implementation at `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/FeatureFlags/EFFeatureFlagOverrideStore.cs:33`); read by the snapshot in a fresh scope per load (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/FeatureFlags/FeatureFlagOverrideSnapshot.cs:104`) and written by the shipped administration service (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/FeatureFlags/FeatureFlagAdministrationService.cs:32`). See [IFeatureFlagOverrideSnapshot](#ifeatureflagoverridesnapshot) and [IFeatureFlagAdministrationService](#ifeatureflagadministrationservice).

### FormFileUploadExtensions
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Uploads` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Uploads/FormFileUploadExtensions.cs:31` · Level 4 · class (static, extension block)

- **What it is**: bounded upload reads for a controller action that takes an `IFormFile`: the size check, the single exact read and the refusal error in one call, instead of a hand-written check plus a `MemoryStream` copy per action (doc comment `FormFileUploadExtensions.cs:26-30`).
- **Depends on**: [UploadedFileContent](#uploadedfilecontent) and [CsvUpload](#csvupload) (the two result shapes); [Result](group-01-result-error-handling.md#result) and [Error](group-01-result-error-handling.md#error); [CsvReader](group-01-result-error-handling.md#csvreader) for the CSV parse; ASP.NET Core `IFormFile`.
- **Concept introduced (bound the file, not only the request).** `[Rubric §11, Security]` and `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]` assess whether untrusted input is bounded before it costs memory. An action's `[RequestSizeLimit]` bounds the request body; this bounds the file itself, and a declared length over the limit is refused before any byte is read (remarks at `FormFileUploadExtensions.cs:45-48`). `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]`: every refused file returns the caller's own `Error` unchanged, so each action keeps its documented error code.
- **Walkthrough**
  - One `extension(IFormFile? file)` block (`FormFileUploadExtensions.cs:33`), so the methods also accept a missing file.
  - `ReadBoundedAsync(maxBytes, invalidUpload)` (`:49`) throws `ArgumentOutOfRangeException` for `maxBytes` below 1 or above `Array.MaxLength` and `ArgumentNullException` for a null error (`:51-53`), then delegates to `ReadBoundedCoreAsync`.
  - `ReadCsvAsync(maxBytes, invalidUpload)` (`:72`) applies the same argument guards (`:74-76`) and delegates to `ReadCsvCoreAsync`.
  - `ReadBoundedCoreAsync` (`:82`) refuses a null, empty or oversize file with `invalidUpload` (`:88-89`), checks cancellation, allocates one `byte[]` of the declared length (`:93`) and fills it with `ReadExactlyAsync` (`:99`). A stream shorter than its declared length raises `EndOfStreamException`, which is answered with the same `invalidUpload` (`:101-104`). Success carries the declared file name and content type (`:107`).
  - `ReadCsvCoreAsync` (`:110`) reuses the bounded read, passing its errors through (`:117-118`), then parses the buffer through a read-only `MemoryStream` with `CsvReader.ReadTableAsync` (`:121-125`). A parse failure returns the reader's own errors unchanged (`MalformedCode` with the line in `Target`, or `EmptyCode`, per the doc comment `:65-69`); success returns a [CsvUpload](#csvupload) (`:127-129`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the public methods validate their arguments synchronously and hand off to private async cores, so a programming error throws at the call rather than inside a faulted task. One buffer of the declared length, read exactly once, is the cheapest correct read; a truncated stream is treated like any other bad upload rather than surfacing as an exception. Every `await` uses `ConfigureAwait(false)` (`:99`, `:116`, `:124`), as packaged non-UI code requires.
- **Where it's used**: ADC actions call it directly: `ReadBoundedAsync` for session assets and user photos (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Conference/MMCA.ADC.Conference.API/Controllers/SessionAssets/SessionAssetsController.cs:197`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Identity/MMCA.ADC.Identity.API/Controllers/UsersController.cs:171`) and `ReadCsvAsync` for the ticket roster import (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Engagement/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.API/Controllers/TicketRosterImportController.cs:77`). Covered by `FormFileUploadExtensionsTests` and `FormFileCsvUploadTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/Uploads/`). A body over the endpoint limit never reaches it: [OversizeFormResourceFilter](#oversizeformresourcefilter) answers 413 first.

### MiddlewarePipelineBuilder
> MMCA.Common.API · `MMCA.Common.API.Startup.Pipeline` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/Pipeline/MiddlewarePipelineBuilder.cs:15` · Level 10 · class (sealed)

- **What it is**: the mutable, ordered list of [MiddlewarePipelineStep](#middlewarepipelinestep) values behind `UseCommonMiddlewarePipeline`. `CreateDefault()` seeds the framework's eighteen-step edge pipeline; a host may then insert, replace, or remove steps by name; and `Build()` re-checks four load-bearing adjacencies before anything is applied.
- **Depends on**: [MiddlewarePipelineStep](#middlewarepipelinestep) and [MiddlewarePipelineStepNames](#middlewarepipelinestepnames); [CorrelationIdMiddleware](#correlationidmiddleware), [TenantResolutionMiddleware](#tenantresolutionmiddleware) and [SoftDeletedUserMiddleware](#softdeletedusermiddleware) (the three custom middlewares it wires); [WebApplicationBuilderExtensions](#webapplicationbuilderextensions) for the two CORS policy names; [JwksEndpointExtensions](#jwksendpointextensions) and [OidcDiscoveryEndpointExtensions](#oidcdiscoveryendpointextensions) for the two always-mapped well-known endpoints; [WebApplicationExtensions](#webapplicationextensions) for `UseCommonRequestLocalization` and the two pre-forwarded `HttpContext.Items` keys; [CommonForwardedHeaders](#commonforwardedheaders) for the shared forwarded-headers options; ASP.NET forwarded-headers primitives.
- **Concept introduced (a customization point that validates itself, not a free-for-all).** The tension a shared pipeline has to resolve: one fixed order is safe but blocks any host with a legitimate extra step, while an open `Action<WebApplication>` hook gives the order back to every host and re-opens exactly the bugs the shared pipeline closed. The resolution here is a **scoped escape hatch with startup-enforced invariants**: the host may edit the list, but four adjacencies are re-asserted afterwards and a violation throws while the host is starting, naming the invariant it broke and printing the current order. `[Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]` assesses whether a change can be made locally without breaking distant behavior; encoding the rationale as an executable check rather than a comment is what makes that true here. `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]` also applies: the failure modes these invariants prevent (an unreachable `jwks_uri`, a tenant that never resolves, a per-user rate cap that never engages) all look like configuration bugs at runtime, so converting them into a startup exception with a named cause is a large operability win. See [ADR-079](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/079-shared-http-middleware-pipeline.html).
- **Walkthrough**
  - One field, `_steps` (`MiddlewarePipelineBuilder.cs:17`), and a private constructor (`:19`), so the only way in is `CreateDefault()`. `StepNames` (`:24`) projects the current names in application order, which is what the fitness function asserts on and what error messages print.
  - `CreateDefault()` (`:31-150`) seeds the eighteen steps. Reading it top to bottom is the fastest way to learn the edge: exception handler (`:34-36`), correlation id (`:38-40`), request localization (`:42-47`, with the ADR-027 note that this runs early so edge error localization uses the caller's culture), the pre-forwarded scheme and host capture (`:49-61`, which stores both values in `HttpContext.Items` before the forwarded-headers rewrite; nothing in the framework reads those two Items today, since the OIDC discovery endpoint derives `jwks_uri` from `Jwt:Issuer`), forwarded headers via [CommonForwardedHeaders](#commonforwardedheaders)`.Create()` clearing `KnownProxies`/`KnownIPNetworks` for cloud reverse proxies (`:63-68`; UI hosts take the same options through `UseCommonUiForwardedHeaders`), an HTTPS redirect wrapped in `UseWhen` that **skips negotiated-h2c requests** via `IsCleartextHttp2` (`:70-86`) so gRPC-over-cleartext calls are not 307-redirected, response compression (`:88-90`), routing (`:92-94`), CORS choosing the development or production policy by environment (`:96-100`), authentication (`:102-104`), tenant resolution (`:106-112`), the rate limiter (`:114-120`), the soft-deleted-user filter (`:122-124`), authorization (`:126-128`), output cache (`:130-132`), the always-mapped JWKS (`:134-141`) and OIDC discovery (`:143-145`) endpoints, and finally `MapControllers()` (`:147-149`). Each `Configure` delegate is `static`, so no closure is allocated per step.
  - `IsCleartextHttp2(HttpContext)` (`:349-355`, public static) is the HTTPS-redirect exemption predicate, defined at the bottom of the class and pulled out to its own method under SEC-Common-44: it null-checks the context (`:351`) and returns true only when `!Request.IsHttps` **and** `HttpProtocol.IsHttp2(Request.Protocol)` (`:353-354`), i.e. the connection negotiated HTTP/2 without TLS. It replaced a `Content-Type: application/grpc` string check, which any caller could forge in a header; the negotiated protocol is settled by Kestrel at connection setup and cannot be faked by a request header. The method is `public` specifically so a host that rebuilds the `HttpsRedirection` step reuses the same predicate instead of reintroducing the forgeable check.
  - Four mutators, all returning `this` for chaining and all validating first: `InsertBefore` (`:160`), `InsertAfter` (`:177`), `Replace` (`:197`) and `Remove` (`:218`). `Replace` keeps the replaced step's position and permits a different name, but rejects a name another step already carries (`:202-208`).
  - `Build()` (`:251`) runs the four checks and returns a defensive copy (`:273`). Two adjacency checks: `PreForwardedCapture` immediately before `ForwardedHeaders` (`:253-256`) and `Authentication` immediately before `TenantResolution` (`:258-261`). Two precedence checks: `Authentication` before `RateLimiting` (`:263-266`, ADR-019) and `ForwardedHeaders` before `HttpsRedirection` (`:268-271`). Every check carries its rationale string, which is what the exception message prints.
  - The private helpers hold the guard semantics. `RequireIndexOf` (`:279`) rejects a blank name and, for an unknown one, throws listing every known step (`:290-293`), which turns a typo into a self-answering error. `RequireUniqueName` (`:298`) enforces name uniqueness across the list. `RequireImmediatelyBefore` (`:308`) and `RequirePrecedes` (`:323`) share one subtle rule: an invariant **binds only when both of its steps are still present** (`:314`, `:329`), so a host that removes a whole capability (both members of a pair) stays legal, while a host that removes only one half is not constrained by a rule that no longer has anything to say.
- **Why it's built this way**: the "both present or the rule is silent" clause is the design decision worth internalizing. Without it, `Remove(MiddlewarePipelineStepNames.TenantResolution)` on a single-tenant host would fail the authentication adjacency check for no reason, and the escape hatch would be unusable. With it, the invariants constrain *reordering* rather than *composition*, which is what they were written to protect. Constructing the defaults as data rather than as calls also means the whole order can be asserted with no `WebApplication` built at all, which is what puts the fitness function in the fast unit tier. The `IsCleartextHttp2` extraction is the same "encode the invariant, don't comment it" instinct the class applies to step ordering: SEC-Common-44 turned a header the caller controls into a protocol fact Kestrel controls, closing a path where a forged `Content-Type` could have served plaintext gRPC traffic that should have been redirected.
- **Where it's used**: only through [WebApplicationExtensions](#webapplicationextensions)`.ApplyPipeline` (`WebApplicationExtensions.cs:140-151`), which both `UseCommonMiddlewarePipeline` overloads route through, so the zero-argument path is exactly the validated default pipeline. `MiddlewarePipelineOrderTestsBase` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Testing/Conformance/MiddlewarePipelineOrderTestsBase.cs:31`) subclasses into each app's architecture tier (ADC, Store, Helpdesk, and Common's own testing tier) and freezes the eighteen-name order; `MiddlewarePipelineBuilderTests` covers the mutators and the invariants directly.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the builder validates order, not semantics. A `Replace` that keeps a step's name but swaps in an unrelated middleware passes every check, because nothing inspects the `Configure` delegate.

### FeatureFlagAssembly
> MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · `MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.FeatureFlags` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/FeatureFlags/FeatureFlagCatalog.cs:9` · Level 0 · record (internal, sealed)

- **What it is**: a one-property DI carrier: "this assembly's `*Features` classes declare flags this host may override" (doc comment, `FeatureFlagCatalog.cs:7-8`).
- **Depends on**: `System.Reflection.Assembly` only.
- **Concept introduced**: none of its own; it is the multi-registration input of [FeatureFlagCatalog](#featureflagcatalog), which teaches the declared-flag catalog. Wrapping a bare `Assembly` in a named record is what lets several modules each contribute one with `AddSingleton` and the catalog take them all as `IEnumerable<FeatureFlagAssembly>`, without colliding with any other `Assembly` registration in the container.
- **Walkthrough**: a positional record, `FeatureFlagAssembly(Assembly Assembly)` (`FeatureFlagCatalog.cs:9`). No members beyond the generated ones.
- **Where it's used**: `AddReloadableFeatureFlags` registers one per assembly passed to it (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.FeatureFlags.cs:52-55`), before its already-registered check, so a second call (one per module) still adds its assemblies. [FeatureFlagCatalog](#featureflagcatalog) consumes the set (`FeatureFlagCatalog.cs:17`).

### FeatureFlagOverrideModelGate
> MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · `MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Persistence.FeatureFlags` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/FeatureFlags/FeatureFlagOverrideModelGate.cs:13` · Level 0 · class (internal, sealed, empty)

- **What it is**: a stateless marker service whose mere presence in DI says "the `FeatureFlagOverrides` table belongs in this host's EF model" (`FeatureFlagOverrideModelGate.cs:3-7`).
- **Depends on**: nothing. Read by [ApplicationDbContext](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#applicationdbcontext).
- **Concept introduced**: the DI-registration-as-model-opt-in gate is taught at [PermissionGrantModelGate](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#permissiongrantmodelgate), which this type names as its precedent (`FeatureFlagOverrideModelGate.cs:5`). `[Rubric §8, Data Architecture]` assesses whether schema follows deliberate ownership; here the DI call IS the opt-in, "so a host that never makes it keeps a byte-identical model and sees no migration" (`:5-6`).
- **Walkthrough**: the declaration is a body-less `internal sealed class FeatureFlagOverrideModelGate;` (`:13`). The behavior lives at its readers. `ApplicationDbContext.ResolveFeatureFlagOverrideGate` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/DbContexts/ApplicationDbContext.cs:1089-1094`) requires both `GetService<FeatureFlagOverrideModelGate>() is not null` and that this context's physical source name equal `FeatureFlagOverrideSettings.DataSourceName` (ordinal compare), so the table lands only in the owning service's own database. `OnModelCreating` then applies [FeatureFlagOverrideModelBuilderExtensions](#featureflagoverridemodelbuilderextensions) first, ahead of the soft-delete and concurrency passes (`ApplicationDbContext.cs:532-538`).
- **Why it's built this way**: `GetService` rather than `GetRequiredService` makes absence mean "not opted in" instead of failing every context construction (`FeatureFlagOverrideModelGate.cs:9-11`).
- **Where it's used**: registered by `AddReloadableFeatureFlags` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.FeatureFlags.cs:70`), which also uses it as its "already registered" marker so the definition provider is never decorated twice (`:57-59`). [DesignTimeDbContextHelper](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#designtimedbcontexthelper) registers it for `dotnet ef` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/DbContexts/Design/DesignTimeDbContextHelper.cs:205`).

### JwtForwardingDelegatingHandler
> MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · `MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Http` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Http/JwtForwardingDelegatingHandler.cs:17` · Level 0 · class (sealed)

- **What it is**: an HTTP `DelegatingHandler` that copies the inbound `Authorization` header from the current `HttpContext` onto every outgoing request of the client it is attached to, so a service-to-service HTTP call travels on the caller's own bearer token.
- **Depends on**: `Microsoft.AspNetCore.Http.IHttpContextAccessor` (constructor-injected, `JwtForwardingDelegatingHandler.cs:17`), `System.Net.Http.Headers.AuthenticationHeaderValue`. Its gRPC twin is [JwtForwardingClientInterceptor](group-13-grpc-contracts.md#jwtforwardingclientinterceptor), which the doc comment names explicitly (`:12`).
- **Concept introduced (token propagation at the transport boundary).** `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses whether authorization survives a hop: the downstream service authorizes the *user*, not the calling service, which means the caller's JWT has to arrive with the request. `[Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]` is the other half: because the forwarding lives in a message handler, application code that calls a typed client is identical whether the module is in-process or extracted, and no handler has to know a token exists. `[Rubric §29, Resilience, Reliability & Business Continuity]`: this is the classic **chain-of-responsibility** shape of `HttpClient`, one concern per handler.
- **Walkthrough**
  - `SendAsync` (`:22`) guards the request (`:24`), then returns straight to `base.SendAsync` in two cases. First, when `request.Headers.Authorization` is already set (`:27-30`), because a previous handler or the caller stated its own credentials and must win. Second, when there is no inbound header to copy (`:32-36`): `httpContextAccessor.HttpContext?.Request?.Headers.Authorization.ToString()` is null-conditional the whole way, so a background processor or an outbox dispatch with no ambient request simply passes through untouched.
  - When there is a value, it normalizes the scheme (`:40-43`): an inbound `"Bearer <token>"` has the prefix sliced off case-insensitively, and anything else is treated as the parameter itself. The result is assigned as a structured `AuthenticationHeaderValue(BearerScheme, token)` (`:45`) rather than as a raw string, so the outgoing header is always well-formed `Bearer <token>` with the constant scheme from `:19`.
- **Why it's built this way**: the no-op-without-`HttpContext` behavior (`:13-14`) is what makes the handler safe to attach unconditionally: background work uses its own credentials and is not accidentally given the last request's token. Slicing and re-forming the scheme instead of copying the string verbatim means a malformed or scheme-less inbound value still leaves the client with a valid header.
- **Where it's used**: wired by the framework's typed-service-client helper, `AddTypedServiceClient<TInterface, TImplementation>` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:155`), which calls `AddHttpContextAccessor()` and `TryAddTransient<JwtForwardingDelegatingHandler>()` (`:147-148`) and then chains `.AddHttpMessageHandler<JwtForwardingDelegatingHandler>()` onto the typed `HttpClient` (`:151-153`) alongside Aspire service discovery and the standard resilience handler (`:156`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: **no shipped app calls `AddTypedServiceClient` today.** The helper's own doc scopes it to HTTP contracts that do not warrant a gRPC binding (webhook receivers, public REST endpoints, third-party wrappers) and points at `MMCA.Common.Grpc.AddTypedGrpcClient` as the preferred service-to-service path (`DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:142-145`), which is the path the cross-service calls in ADC and Store actually take. This handler is therefore live framework code on a currently unused registration path.

### ICorrelationContext
> MMCA.Common.Application · `MMCA.Common.Application.Interfaces` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Interfaces/ICorrelationContext.cs:8` · Level 0 · interface

- **What it is**: the Application-layer abstraction for "the correlation ID of the work currently in flight": one reader and one writer, resolved per scope.
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party, BCL `string` only. Implemented by [CorrelationContext](#correlationcontext) in Infrastructure.
- **Concept introduced (request correlation as an injected ambient value).** `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]` assesses whether one logical request can be reassembled from disjoint logs; a **correlation ID** is the value that makes that possible. `[Rubric §3, Clean Architecture]` is the reason this interface exists at all: handlers, decorators and interceptors need the ID, but they must not reach for `HttpContext`, so the contract sits in Application and the holder sits in Infrastructure, keeping the dependency arrow pointing inward. `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]`: nothing in the business path sets or threads the ID, it is populated once at the edge and read wherever it is needed.
- **Walkthrough**: two members. `string CorrelationId { get; }` (`ICorrelationContext.cs:11`) is read-only to consumers. `void SetCorrelationId(string correlationId)` (`ICorrelationContext.cs:15`) is the single write path, on the same interface, so the edge component that populates the value can do it through the DI-resolved instance without a second, internal interface. The doc comment (`ICorrelationContext.cs:3-6`) states the intended lifecycle: set by middleware from the `X-Correlation-ID` header or generated, then carried through the handler pipeline in structured-logging scopes.
- **Why it's built this way**: a two-member interface is small enough that a test or a background host can supply its own holder, and putting the setter here (rather than on the concrete class) keeps middleware depending on the abstraction. [ITenantContext](group-05-cqrs-pipeline.md#itenantcontext) is deliberately modelled on it, and says so: one scoped instance per request, populated once at the edge (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Interfaces/ITenantContext.cs:11`).
- **Where it's used**: registered as `TryAddScoped<ICorrelationContext, CorrelationContext>()` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.cs:342`). Populated by [CorrelationIdMiddleware](#correlationidmiddleware), which takes it as a method-injected parameter of `InvokeAsync` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Middleware/CorrelationIdMiddleware.cs:35`). Consumed by [LoggingCommandDecorator<TCommand, TResult>](group-05-cqrs-pipeline.md#loggingcommanddecoratortcommand-tresult) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/UseCases/Decorators/LoggingCommandDecorator.cs:20`) and [LoggingQueryDecorator<TQuery, TResult>](group-05-cqrs-pipeline.md#loggingquerydecoratortquery-tresult) (`.../LoggingQueryDecorator.cs:16`), which wrap the ID into their log scope for the duration of the pipeline.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: not every component can use it. The audit-trail interceptor is a singleton and records the ambient `Activity` trace id instead of this scoped value, and says so in its own comment (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/AuditTrail/AuditTrailEntry.cs:94`).

### OutboundAddressPolicy
> MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · `MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Http` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Http/OutboundAddressPolicy.cs:11` · Level 0 · class (public, static)

- **What it is**: the address and target rules an outbound call to an operator-supplied URL is held to, so a stored target cannot reach the host's own network (`OutboundAddressPolicy.cs:5-9`). Two public predicates: `IsPublicAddress` and `IsAcceptableTargetUri`.
- **Depends on**: BCL only (`System.Net.IPAddress`, `System.Net.Sockets.AddressFamily`, `System.Uri`).
- **Concept introduced (server-side request forgery defense).** `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses whether the system refuses attacker-steerable requests to internal resources. **SSRF** is the attack where a server is talked into calling a URL the attacker chose (a webhook target, say) that resolves into the private network, loopback, or the cloud host's metadata endpoint. This class is the single rule set for that check, applied twice by its callers: once to the URI as written and once to every address the name resolves to at connect time, which is what defeats DNS rebinding.
- **Walkthrough**
  - `DeniedIPv4` (`:18-32`) is a deny-list of (network, prefix) pairs: `0/8`, `10/8`, `100.64/10`, `127/8`, `169.254/16`, `172.16/12`, `192.0.0.0/24`, `192.168/16`, `198.18/15`, `224/4`, `240/4`, plus the single address `168.63.129.16/32` (`:31`), the cloud platform's host endpoint that is reachable from every VM although it is public space (`:13-17`).
  - IPv6 is an allow-list instead: only global unicast `2000::/3` (`GlobalUnicastIPv6`, `:40-41`), because several formats outside it embed an IPv4 address the stack may still dial (IPv4-compatible `::/96`, both NAT64 prefixes) and a range added later is refused without anyone listing it (`:34-39`). Inside it, `DeniedGlobalIPv6` (`:48-54`) still refuses Teredo `2001::/32`, 6to4 `2002::/16` and the documentation prefixes `2001:db8::/32` and `3fff::/20`.
  - `IsPublicAddress` (`:68`) unwraps an IPv4-mapped IPv6 address to its IPv4 form first (`:72-75`), checks IPv4 against the deny-list (`:77-80`), returns false for any family that is neither IPv4 nor IPv6 (`:82-85`), and for IPv6 requires "inside `2000::/3` and in none of the denied ranges" (`:87-89`).
  - `IsAcceptableTargetUri(target, allowInsecure)` (`:100`) always requires the callable shape: absolute, `https` or `http`, no user info, a non-empty host (`HasCallableShape`, `:114-118`). Unless `allowInsecure` (Development only, per the parameter doc), it also requires `https`, a host that is not `localhost` or `*.localhost` (`IsLocalHostName`, `:123-125`), and, for an IP-literal host only, a public address (`IsPublicIfLiteral`, `:128-130`); a host name passes here because its addresses are checked when it is resolved (`:127`).
  - `IsInRange` (`:135-151`) compares the whole prefix bytes with `SequenceEqual`, then masks the remaining bits of the next byte (`:149`).
- **Why it's built this way**: one public static class means the validation-time and connect-time checks cannot drift apart. [ADR-142](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/142-outbound-webhooks-and-delivery-lane.html) covers the outbound-webhook design this serves.
- **Where it's used**: [DnsWebhookTargetValidator](group-04-events-outbox.md#dnswebhooktargetvalidator) applies `IsAcceptableTargetUri` and then requires every resolved address to pass `IsPublicAddress` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/DnsWebhookTargetValidator.cs:31`, `:52`). [HttpWebhookSender](group-04-events-outbox.md#httpwebhooksender) re-checks the URI before sending (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/HttpWebhookSender.cs:46`), and [WebhookConnectCallback](group-04-events-outbox.md#webhookconnectcallback) refuses a non-public address at connect time (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookConnectCallback.cs:48`).

### RejectedLease
> Linked source (compiled into each limiter host package) · `MMCA.Common.RateLimiting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Build/RateLimiting/RemainingWindowFixedWindowRateLimiter.cs:137` · Level 0 · class (private, sealed, nested)

- **What it is**: the rejection lease [RemainingWindowFixedWindowRateLimiter](#remainingwindowfixedwindowratelimiter) hands back in place of the framework's: not acquired, carrying only a remaining-window `Retry-After` hint (`RemainingWindowFixedWindowRateLimiter.cs:132-136`).
- **Depends on**: `System.Threading.RateLimiting.RateLimitLease` (base), `MetadataName.RetryAfter`.
- **Concept introduced**: none of its own; see the parent type for the honest `Retry-After` concept.
- **Walkthrough**: a primary constructor takes the `TimeSpan retryAfter` (`:137`). `IsAcquired` is constant `false` (`:142`). `MetadataNames` returns a static one-element array holding `MetadataName.RetryAfter.Name` (`:139`, `:145`). `TryGetMetadata` answers that name, compared ordinally, with the captured `retryAfter` (`:148-154`) and every other name with `null`/`false` (`:156-157`).
- **Where it's used**: created only by the parent's `WithRemainingRetryAfter` (`:129`). The rate-limiter `OnRejected` callback turns its hint into the 429's `Retry-After` header (`:134-135`).

### RemainingWindowFixedWindowRateLimiter
> Linked source (compiled into MMCA.Common.API, Aspire, UI.Web and Gateway) · `MMCA.Common.RateLimiting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Build/RateLimiting/RemainingWindowFixedWindowRateLimiter.cs:31` · Level 1 · class (internal, sealed)

- **What it is**: the in-memory fixed-window limiter with an honest `Retry-After` hint. The framework's `FixedWindowRateLimiter` stamps every rejection with the whole window length, so a 429 always said `Retry-After: 60` however much of the minute was left; this wraps it, tracks when the current window opened, and rewrites the hint to the time remaining (`RemainingWindowFixedWindowRateLimiter.cs:5-12`).
- **Depends on**: `System.Threading.RateLimiting` (`ReplenishingRateLimiter` base, an inner `FixedWindowRateLimiter`), `TimeProvider`. Nested [RejectedLease](#rejectedlease). Its distributed counterpart is [RedisFixedWindowRateLimiter](#redisfixedwindowratelimiter), whose figure this one now matches (`:10-11`).
- **Concept introduced (decorating a BCL limiter to correct its metadata).** `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]` assesses whether responses tell clients the truth they need to behave well: a `Retry-After` that overstates the wait makes well-behaved clients back off longer than necessary. `[Rubric §2, Design Patterns]`: a **decorator** over `FixedWindowRateLimiter` that forwards every permit decision unchanged and rewrites only the rejection metadata.
- **Walkthrough**
  - The constructor (`:45`) copies the options into a new inner `FixedWindowRateLimiter` with `AutoReplenishment = false` forced (`:49-56`), because the owning partitioned limiter drives replenishment. It sets `ReplenishmentPeriod` to the window (`:57`), takes `TimeProvider.System` when none is injected (`:58`), and stamps the window start (`:59`).
  - `IsAutoReplenishing` is `false` (`:63`); `IdleDuration` and `GetStatistics` forward to the inner limiter (`:69`, `:72`).
  - `TryReplenish` (`:75`) resets its own window start when at least one period has elapsed (`:77-80`), then delegates (`:82`). The remarks explain why this agrees with the inner limiter's own window to within one timer tick (`:23-28`).
  - `TimeUntilNextWindow` (`:87-91`) is period minus elapsed, clamped at zero.
  - `AttemptAcquireCore` and `AcquireAsyncCore` (`:94`, `:98`) both pass the inner lease through `WithRemainingRetryAfter` (`:121`): an acquired lease, or a rejection with no `RetryAfter` hint (a request larger than the whole allowance, `:119-120`), is returned untouched (`:123-126`); any other rejection is disposed and replaced by a [RejectedLease](#rejectedlease) carrying `TimeUntilNextWindow()` (`:128-129`).
  - `Dispose(bool)` and `DisposeAsyncCore` dispose the inner limiter before the base (`:102-117`).
- **Why it's built this way**: one source file is linked as an internal type into each package that runs an in-memory fixed window, keeping the Gateway package free of any MMCA reference; it cannot sit in `MMCA.Common.Shared` because `System.Threading.RateLimiting` ships in the ASP.NET Core shared framework, which Shared (also loaded by Blazor WebAssembly) does not reference (`:13-22`). [ADR-088](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/088-gateway-edge-responsibilities.html) covers the gateway edge limiter it serves.
- **Where it's used**: every in-memory fixed-window partition: [RateLimitPartitions](#ratelimitpartitions) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/RateLimiting/RateLimitPartitions.cs:48`), `AddCommonRateLimiting` in [WebApplicationBuilderExtensions](#webapplicationbuilderextensions) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.RateLimiting.cs:312`), the gateway edge limiter (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Gateway/GatewayRateLimitingExtensions.cs:329`), the gateway route policies (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/RateLimiting/GatewayRoutePolicyExtensions.cs:145`) and the Blazor host's edge limiter (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.UI.Web/Hardening/UiRateLimitingExtensions.cs:167`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the class doc cites a Store local test run ("X-18", `:9`) as the trigger; that run is not in source.

### FeatureFlagDefinitions
> MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · `MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.FeatureFlags` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/FeatureFlags/FeatureFlagDefinitions.cs:19` · Level 2 · class (internal, static)

- **What it is**: the two-way translation between a stored override and the `Microsoft.FeatureManagement` `FeatureDefinition` the feature manager evaluates, plus the summary of a definition into the coarse state the administration surface reports.
- **Depends on**: [FeatureFlagOverrideResponse](#featureflagoverrideresponse), [FeatureFlagOverrideMode](#featureflagoverridemode), [FeatureFlagValueKind](#featureflagvaluekind); `Microsoft.FeatureManagement` (`FeatureDefinition`, `FeatureFilterConfiguration`, `RequirementType`, `FeatureStatus`), `Microsoft.Extensions.Configuration` in-memory builder.
- **Concept introduced (stored overrides expressed as ordinary filter definitions).** `[Rubric §2, Design Patterns]`: rather than a second evaluation path, an override is turned into the same `FeatureDefinition` configuration would have produced, so the feature manager and every FeatureGate decorator stay unaware overrides exist.
